(Tail bounds for the distribution of
D
)
.

Let
D
be as in (
2.4
). Then,

(5.6)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)}.

Furthermore, assume that
b
and
d
are such that Assumption (
F-D
) is satisfied. When
\rho_{2}
diverges and
d=o(b)
,

(5.7)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-(\frac{1}{2}+o(1))\rho_{2}(k)}.

If, instead, Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied
(
i.e.
\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{2}(k)
exists
)
,

(5.8)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-\mathcal{O}(1)}.

5.2.
Remaining lifetime and large degrees

The lifetime equals
L=S_{D+1}
. We first study distributional properties of the
remaining lifetime
, defined as

(5.9)

S_{D+1}-S_{k}\text{ conditionally on }D\geq k\qquad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

The remaining lifetime is equal to
L
when
k=0
, but equals the amount of time an individual has left to live after producing
k
children when
k>0
. Conditionally on
\{D\geq k\}
, let
D_{k}:=D-k
denote the
excess offspring
. It is clear that
D_{k}
has distribution

(5.10)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}=\ell\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=k+\ell\,|\,D\geq k\right)=\frac{d(k+\ell)}{b(k+\ell)+d(k+\ell)}\prod_{i=k}^{k+\ell-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)},\qquad\text{for }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

That is,
D_{k}
is an inhomogeneous geometric random variable, characterised by the sequences
(b(k+\ell))_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(d(k+\ell))_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Upon
\{D\geq k\}
we can write
D=k+D_{k}
. Hence, the remaining lifetime after giving birth to
k
children equals

(5.11)

\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}.

Regarding this random variable, we have the following results.

Lemma 5.5

(Remaining lifetime)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied. Suppose there exists
I\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
d^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
d(i)=d^{*}
for all
i\geq I
. Then,

(5.12)

\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(d^{*})\quad\text{for any }k\geq I.

In particular, when
I=0
so that
d\equiv d^{*}
, then
L\sim\mathrm{Exp}(d^{*})
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\lambda>0
, and let
E_{\lambda}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(\lambda)
. Suppose that
d(i)\geq\lambda

(
resp.
d(i)\leq\lambda)
for all
i\geq k
. Then,

(5.13)

\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\preceq E_{\lambda}\qquad\bigg(\text{resp.\ }\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\succeq E_{\lambda}\bigg).

Finally, when
b
and
d
are such that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied but Assumption (
F-D
) is not,

(5.14)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}=\infty\right)>0\qquad\text{for any }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

When
d
converges to some value
d^{*}\in(0,\infty)
, the above result suggests that the remaining lifetime, after having giving birth to a large number of children, is roughly exponentially distributed with rate
d^{*}
. Similarly, when
d
converges to zero, the remaining lifetime has sub-exponential tails and even equals infinity with positive probability when Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied. We make this intuition precise and build on this even further when studying the
entire
lifetime in the next section. For now, we discuss the limiting distribution of the remaining lifetime when
d
diverges, in which case the remaining lifetime has super-exponential tails.

Lemma 5.6

(Remaining lifetime for diverging
d
)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
both tend to infinity, such that
d
is increasing,
d=o(b)
, and
b(k)=o(kd(k))
, and that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied. Furthermore, assume that both
b(k)
and
d(k)
are regularly varying in
k
with a non-negative exponent. Let
t_{k}
be such that
\liminf_{k\to\infty}t_{k}>0
and
t_{k}=o(kd(k)/b(k))
. Then,

(5.15)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>t_{k}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-(1+o(1))t_{k}}.

In particular, with
E
a rate-one exponential random variable,

(5.16)

d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}E,\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

Remark 5.7
.

It is possible to relax the assumption that
d
is increasing by using similar quantities
\overline{d}_{k}
and
\underline{d}_{k}
throughout the proof, and assuming that
d(k)=(1+o(1))\inf_{i\geq k}d(i)
in the case that
d
is slowly varying (i.e. regularly varying with exponent
0
). We used the assumption on
d
to ease notation and aid the reader, as well as since applications of Lemma
5.6
require only increasing death rates (see Section
8
).
\blacktriangleleft

Remark 5.8

(Linear birth rates)
.

We can weaken the assumption that
t_{k}=o(kd(k)/b(k))
somewhat in the case that
b(k)=\mathcal{O}(k)
. Namely, we can assume that
t_{k}=\mathcal{O}(d(k))
. This weaker assumption is required in the proof of Proposition
8.3
. See Remark
A.1
in Appendix
A
for more details on how to adjust the proof.
\blacktriangleleft

So far, we have either looked at the distribution of the offspring
D
or the remaining lifetime separately. We are also interested in events that combine both quantities, in particular how likely (or unlikely) it is for an individual to survive for a long time and obtain a large offspring. By Corollary
5.1
, we can write the event that the root has degree at least
k
at time
t
and is alive at time
t^{\prime}\geq t
as

(5.17)

\{d(t)\geq k,L>t^{\prime}\}=\{D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\}.

We then have the following result.

Lemma 5.9

(Survival with a high degree)
.

(i)
Suppose that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
F-D
) are satisfied. Suppose that there exist
x\geq 0
and
K=K(x)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
d(i)\geq x
for all
i\geq K
. Then, for all
k\geq K
and all
t^{\prime}\geq t\geq 0
,

(5.18)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-x(t^{\prime}-t)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathrm{e}^{x(S_{k}-t)}\right].

(ii)
Suppose that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
F-D
) are satisfied. Suppose that there exist
x\geq 0
and
K=K(x)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(i)\leq x
for all
i\geq K
. Then, for all
k\geq K
and all
t^{\prime}\geq t\geq 0
,

(5.19)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-x(t^{\prime}-t)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathrm{e}^{x(S_{k}-t)}\right].

(iii)
Suppose that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied but Assumption (
F-D
) is not. Then,

(5.20)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)=\Theta(\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t\right))\qquad\text{for any $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$ and $t^{\prime}\geq t\geq 0$,}

where the constants in the
\Theta
notation are independent of
k,t^{\prime}
, and
t
.

To use the bounds presented in Lemma
5.9
, the asymptotic behaviour of the expected values is required (for a suitable choice of
t
). To this end, we present the following result from
[
2
]
, regarding moderate deviations of sums of independent exponential random variables.

Lemma 5.10

(Moderate deviation principle, Lemma
7.10

[
2
]
)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{N}_{0}\to(0,\infty)
and assume
f(k)
tends to infinity with
k
. Let
(E_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of independent exponential random variables, where
E_{i}
has rate
f(i)
, and set
S_{k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}E_{i}
. Assume that
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\rm Var}(S_{k})=\infty
. Then, for
z\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{{\rm Var}(S_{k})}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]\geq z{\rm Var}(S_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{z^{2}}{2},

and

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{{\rm Var}(S_{k})}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]\leq-z{\rm Var}(S_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{z^{2}}{2}.

Remark 5.11
.

We shall use Lemma
5.10
throughout with
f\equiv b+d
, so that
\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]=\varphi_{1}(k)
and
{\rm Var}(S_{k})=\varphi_{2}(k)
.
\blacktriangleleft

We extend this result by including exponential weighting or tilting of the sum
S_{k}
. We phrase the following result in terms of the sequences
b
and
d
for ease of writing, since we apply this in combination with Lemma
5.9
in the analysis in Section
7
.

Proposition 5.12

(Moderate deviation principle with exponential tilting)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that
b
diverges and that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
D-V
) are satisfied. Then, for
z>0
and
y,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.21)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varphi_{2}(k)}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta(S_{k}-(\varphi_{1}(k)-y\varphi_{2}(k)))}\right]=\theta(y-z)-\frac{z^{2}}{2}.

5.3.
Functional inequalities

We conclude this section with a few elementary results regarding the functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
, and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, as well as
\alpha
and
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
, as introduced in Section
2
.

Lemma 5.13

(Functional inequalities)
.

(a)

Suppose that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied and
b
tends to infinity. Then,
\varphi_{2}=o(\varphi_{1})
and
\mathcal{K}(t)=o(t)
. Similarly, suppose that Assumption (
F-D
) is satisfied and
d=o(b)
. Then,
\rho_{2}=o(\rho_{1})
.

(b)

Suppose that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied and that
\overline{d}:=\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i)<\infty
. Then,
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\rho_{1}(k)/\varphi_{1}(k)\leq\overline{d}
. In particular, when
d
converges to zero, then
\rho_{1}=o(\varphi_{1})
. Similarly, if
\underline{d}:=\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i)>0
, then
\liminf_{k\to\infty}\rho_{1}(k)/\varphi_{1}(k)\geq\underline{d}
. In particular, if
d
tends to infinity, then
\varphi_{1}=o(\rho_{1})
.

(c)

Suppose that Assumption (
D-V
) is satisfied and
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,\infty)
. Then,
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
.

(d)

Suppose that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied and
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,\infty)
. Then,
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)=o(t)
. Moreover, for any function
s\colon(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
s
tends to infinity and
s(t)=o(t)
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-s(t))=o(s(t))
.

6.
The oldest alive individual

In this section, we study the quantity
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
; the birth-time of the oldest alive individual in
\mathrm{BP}(t)
conditionally on survival. To this end, we first obtain precise asymptotic distributional properties of the lifetime
L
of individuals in the branching process. We then study the growth-rate of the entire branching process, to finally prove when the oldest individual alive at time
t
is born.

6.1.
Lifetime distribution

In the previous section, we obtained results for the remaining lifetime after giving birth to a number of children. Here, we use those results to now focus on the
entire
lifetime.

The first result of Lemma
5.5
already shows that the lifetime is exponentially distributed when the death rates are constant (and non-zero). We now provide some further asymptotic results when the death rates are not constant. Here we observe that the lifetime distribution undergoes a phase transition between the ‘rich are old‘ and the ‘rich die young’ regimes. This is made precise in the following result.

Lemma 6.1

(Asymptotic exponential lifetime distribution)
.

Assume that the sequences
b
and
d
are such that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied. Recall
R
from (
2.13
). If
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)\geq R
, then

(6.1)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)=-R.

Moreover, when
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
,

(6.2)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\big[\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+Rt\big]>-\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ \limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+Rt}{\log t}<\infty.

If
\overline{d}:=\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i)<R
and
b
tends to infinity, then

(6.3)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq-\overline{d}.

In particular, if
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,R)
exists and
b
tends to infinity,

(6.4)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)=-d^{*}.

Remark 6.2
.

Whilst (
6.1
) holds in a slightly more general setting, we need the more precise results in (
6.2
) in later analysis. We included both results, as their proof are relatively short. Furthermore, the bounds in (
6.2
) are optimal, in the sense that the rescaling is of the correct order in both the liminf and limsup.
\blacktriangleleft

In the case that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}<R
, we can also provide higher-order asymptotic behaviour.

Lemma 6.3

(Third-order asymptotic lifetime distribution)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
F-D
) are satisfied, and recall
R
from (
2.13
). Also suppose that
b
diverges and that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,R)
. Recall the functions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
from (
2.10
), and suppose that Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and Assumption (
D-V
) are satisfied. Then,

(6.5)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+d^{*}t+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)}{\mathcal{K}(t)}=0.

Finally, we consider the behaviour of the lifetime when Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied but Assumption (
F-D
) is not. This is a direct application of (
5.14
) in Lemma
5.5
when
k=0
.

Corollary 6.4

(Infinite lifetime)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied, but Assumption (
F-D
) is not. Then,
\mathbb{P}\!\left(L=\infty\right)>0
.

Lemma
6.1
provides several general results in case Assumption (
N-E
) holds. The ‘rich die young’ and ‘rich are old’ regimes correspond to the results in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), and the results in (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), respectively, and we make the heuristic explanation of (
4.2
) in Section
4
precise in this Lemma. The result in (
6.4
) is then strengthened to a third order asymptotic expansion under additional assumptions in Lemma
6.3
. Here, Assumption (
D-V
) is crucial, whereas Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
are used for technical reasons. This precise result is necessary to study the behaviour of
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
, among others. Finally, Corollary
6.4
provides necessary and sufficient conditions for individuals to have an infinite lifetime with positive probability, which is used in the proof of Theorem
2.3
.

We now prove the two lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

We start by proving (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), and first prove a general lower bound. Suppose there exists
I\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
b(I)+d(I)=R
. Since
L\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}S_{D+1}
by Corollary
5.1
,

(6.6)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{D+1}>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I-1,S_{I}>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I-1\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(E_{I}>t\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I-1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-Rt}.

Note that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I-1\right)
has strictly positive probability, since
b(i)>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Hence, there exists
C>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

(6.7)

\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+Rt\geq-C.

When
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
, the existence of such an index
I
is guaranteed, which yields the first bound in (
6.2
). The lower bound in (
6.7
) also implies that

(6.8)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq-R,

which yields the desired lower bound for (
6.1
) in the case the index
I
exists.

Now suppose such an index
I
does not exist. It follows that
b(j)+d(j)>R:=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(b(i)+d(i))
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so that
\liminf_{i\to\infty}b(i)+d(i)=R
must hold. Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
I^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
b(I^{\prime})+d(I^{\prime})\leq R+\varepsilon
. Then, by the same argument,

(6.9)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I^{\prime}-1\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(E_{I^{\prime}}>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I^{\prime}-1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-(R+\varepsilon)t}.

Again, the probability on the right-hand side is strictly positive, so that

(6.10)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq-(R+\varepsilon).

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, the desired lower bound follows. We remark that the bound in (
6.8
) is thus true in general and that we have not used the assumption that
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)\geq R
for the proof (only for the stronger bound in (
6.7
) do we use that
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
), as it is indeed not necessary. Though true in general, the lower bound in (
6.8
) need not be sharp in case this assumption is not met. The assumption
is
necessary for the upper bound, which we prove now.

For an upper bound, let
I\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
d(i)>R-\varepsilon
for all
i\geq I
, which exists since
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)\geq R
. Then,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{I}>t\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{I}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{I}>t\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I,S_{I}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t\right).

We start by bounding the first probability on the right-hand side. Without loss of generality we can assume that
I\neq 0
. As
b(i)+d(i)\geq R
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
G(I,R)
denote a sum of
I
i.i.d. exponential random variables with rate
R
, to bound

(6.12)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{I}>t\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(G(I,R)>t\right)=\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{R^{I}}{(I-1)!}s^{I-1}\mathrm{e}^{-Rs}\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1+o(1)}{(I-1)!}(Rt)^{I-1}\mathrm{e}^{-Rt}.

We then bound the second probability on the right-hand side. By the choice of
I
and using Lemma
5.9
, we arrive at the upper bound

(6.13)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I,S_{I}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-(R-\varepsilon)t}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{(R-\varepsilon)S_{I}}\right].

Combined, we thus arrive at

(6.14)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\leq\frac{1+o(1)}{(I-1)!}(Rt)^{I-1}\mathrm{e}^{-Rt}+\mathrm{e}^{-(R-\varepsilon)t}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{(R-\varepsilon)S_{I}}\right].

By the definition of
R
and since
I
is finite, the expected value equals a constant
C>0
, independent of
t
. We thus conclude that

(6.15)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\leq-(R-\varepsilon),

Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, combining this with (
6.8
) yields (
6.1
).

We then prove the second bound in (
6.2
). As we assume
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
, we observe that we can choose
\varepsilon=1/t
to depend on
t
and that the index
I\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
b(i)+d(i)>R-\varepsilon=R-1/t
for all
i\geq I
still exists and is
independent
of
t
. Then, the upper bound in (
6.14
) changes to

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+o(1)}{(I-1)!}(Rt)^{I-1}\mathrm{e}^{-Rt}+\mathrm{e}^{-Rt+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{(R-1/t)S_{I}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1+o(1)}{(I-1)!}(Rt)^{I-1}\mathrm{e}^{-Rt}+\mathrm{e}^{-Rt+1}(Rt)^{|[I-1]_{R}|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
i\not\in[I-1]_{R}\end{subarray}}^{I-1}\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)-R+1/t},

where

(6.17)

[I-1]_{R}:=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,I-1\}:b(i)+d(i)=R\}.

The product on the right-hand side can be bounded from above by a constant
C>0
, since
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
and hence

(6.18)

\inf\{b(i)+d(i):i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\setminus[I-1]_{R}\}>R.

As
|[I-1]_{R}|\leq I
, we thus arrive at the upper bound

(6.19)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+Rt}{\log t}\leq I,

which concludes the proof of (
6.2
).

We continue by proving (
6.3
). We let
I=I(\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
d(i)\leq\overline{d}+\varepsilon
for all
i\geq I
. Then,

(6.20)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{D+1}>t,D\geq I\right)=\mathbb{P}\bigg(S_{I}+\!\!\sum_{i=I}^{I+D_{I}}\!\!\!E_{i}>t\bigg)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sum_{i=I}^{I+D_{I}}\!\!\!E_{i}>t\bigg)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I\right).

Since
b(i)>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq I\right)>0
. Then, by Lemma
5.5
, we can bound the other term on the right-hand side from below by
\exp(-(\overline{d}+\varepsilon)t)
, so that

(6.21)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq-(\overline{d}+\varepsilon).

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we arrive at (
6.3
).

We finally prove (
6.4
). It remains to prove an upper bound, as (
6.3
) now holds with
\overline{d}=d^{*}
. For
d^{*}=0
, an upper bound readily follows from the fact that
\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\leq 0
. For
d^{*}>0
, the argument that leads to the upper bound in (
6.15
) is still valid when applying the Chernoff bound with a parameter
\theta\in(0,d^{*}-\varepsilon)
and any
\varepsilon\in(0,d^{*})
, in the sense that there exists
I\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
d(i)>d^{*}-\varepsilon
for all
i\geq I
. The remainder of the argument can be repeated with this
I
and by substituting
d^{*}-\varepsilon
for
R-\varepsilon
. Hence, in this case,

(6.22)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\leq-d^{*}.

Combined with the lower bound in (
6.3
), this proves (
6.4
) and concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Lower bound.
We fix
x>0
and set
k:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t-x\mathcal{K}(t))\rceil
. If
\rho_{2}
tends to infinity, by Lemma
5.4
,

(6.23)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}>t\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-(\frac{1}{2}+o(1))\rho_{2}(k)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}>t\right).

In case
\rho_{2}
converges instead, then
\rho_{2}(k)=o(\varphi_{2}(k))
since
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity. Hence, we can use the lower bound in (
6.23
) with
\rho_{2}(k)
replaced by
o(\varphi_{2}(k))
. Now, by the choice of
k
and using Assumption
\mathcal{K}
and
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
, for any
\varepsilon>0
and all
t
sufficiently large,

(6.24)

t\leq\varphi_{1}(k)+x\mathcal{K}(t)=\varphi_{1}(k)+x\varphi_{2}(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t))\leq\varphi_{1}(k)+(x+\varepsilon/3)\varphi_{2}(k).

Hence,

(6.25)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}>t\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}>\varphi_{1}(k)+(x+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{3})\varphi_{2}(k)\right)\geq\exp\big(-(\tfrac{1}{2}x^{2}+\varepsilon)\varphi_{2}(k)\big),

where the last step follows from Lemma
5.10
for
\varepsilon
small and
k
sufficiently large. Since
d
converges to
d^{*}
, we have
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by
(c)
in Lemma
5.13
. Combined with (
6.25
) in (
6.23
), we obtain the lower bound

(6.26)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\geq\exp\big(-\rho_{1}(k)-\tfrac{1}{2}(x^{2}+(d^{*})^{2}+2\varepsilon+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)\big).

We now write
\rho_{1}(k)=d^{*}\varphi_{1}(k)+\alpha(k)
and use that, since
k=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t-x\mathcal{K}(t))\rceil
, it follows that
|\alpha(k)-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)|=o(\mathcal{K}(t))
by Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and that
\mathcal{K}(t)=(1+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by Assumption
\mathcal{K}
and
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
. This yields, by the choice of
k
,

(6.27)

\displaystyle\exp{}

\displaystyle\big(-d^{*}\varphi_{1}(k)-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)-\tfrac{1}{2}(x^{2}+(d^{*})^{2}+2\varepsilon+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)-\big[\tfrac{1}{2}(x-d^{*})^{2}+\varepsilon+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)\big)

We maximise the lower bound by choosing
x=d^{*}
, so that we arrive at

(6.28)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))+d^{*}t+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)}{\mathcal{K}(t)}\geq-\varepsilon.

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrarily, this yields the desired lower bound.

Upper bound.
We fix
\varepsilon,\zeta>0
small and define

(6.29)

M:=\Big\lceil\frac{1}{\zeta}\Big(\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{\mathcal{K}(t)}\Big)\Big\rceil,\quad x_{i}=i\zeta,\quad\text{and}\quad k_{i}:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t-x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t))\rceil,

where
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,M\}
. Then, we partition
[0,\infty)
into the intervals
[k_{i+1},k_{i})
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,M\}
and
[k_{0},\infty)
(with
k_{M+1}:=0
) to obtain the upper bound

(6.30)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{0}\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k_{M}}>t\right)+\sum_{i=0}^{M-1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{i+1},S_{k_{i}}>t\right).

We bound each term separately.

First term.
By using Lemma
5.4
, we arrive at

(6.31)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{0}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k_{0})}.

We use that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are increasing, the definition of
\alpha
in (
2.9
), and the fact that
d
converges to
d^{*}
, so that
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by
(c)
in Lemma
5.13
, to bound the exponent from above by

(6.32)

\exp\big(-\rho_{1}(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t))-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t))\big)=\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)-(\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

Second term.
A Chernoff bound with
\theta\in(d^{*},R)
such that
\theta(1-\varepsilon)>d^{*}
(which is possible for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small) combined with the inequality
\log(1+x)\leq x-x^{2}/2+x^{3}/3
yields

(6.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k_{M}}\!>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\theta t}\prod_{i=0}^{k_{M}-1}\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\!\!-\theta t+\!\!\sum_{i=0}^{k_{M}-1}\!\bigg[\frac{\theta}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\theta}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\Big(\frac{\theta}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}\Big)^{3}\bigg]\bigg).

Since

(6.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k_{M}-1}{}

\displaystyle\frac{\theta}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{k_{M}-1}\Big(\frac{1}{b(i)+d(i)-\theta}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\theta\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)-\frac{\theta^{4}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{k_{M}-1}\frac{1}{(b(i)+d(i))^{2}(b(i)+d(i)-\theta)^{2}}\leq\theta\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k),

we arrive at the upper bound

(6.35)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k_{M}}>t\right)\leq\exp\big(-\theta t+\theta\varphi_{1}(k_{M})+(\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k_{M})\big).

By the choice of
k_{M}\leq\varphi_{1}^{-1}(\varepsilon t)\leq\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t)
and the fact that
\varphi_{2}=o(\varphi_{1})
since
b
diverges, and hence
\mathcal{K}(t)=o(t)
, we arrive at the upper bound

(6.36)

\exp\big(-((1-\varepsilon)\theta+o(1))t\big).

Since
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)=o(t)
by
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
, it follows that this lower bound is much smaller than
\exp(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t))
.

Summands.
Combining the bounds of the first and second term in each of the summands in (
6.30
) (with again
\theta\in(d^{*},R)
), we arrive at

(6.37)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{i+1},S_{k_{i}}>t\right)\leq\exp\big(-\rho_{1}(k_{i+1})-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k_{i+1})-\theta t+\theta\varphi_{1}(k_{i})+(\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k_{i})\big).

Again using that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are increasing and that
\varphi_{1}(k_{i})=t-x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)+o(1)
as
b
diverges and
\varphi_{2}(k_{i+1})=(1+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k_{i})=(1+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t-x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t))
by Assumption
\mathcal{K}
and
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
, where the
o(1)
is uniform in
i
, we obtain the upper bound

(6.38)

\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t))+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t-x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t))-(\theta-d^{*})x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)+\zeta d^{*}\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

We distinguish between two cases, for which we first introduce the following functions. We define

(6.39)

\widetilde{\alpha}(k)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{|d(i)-d^{*}|}{b(i)+d(i)}\qquad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and extend
\widetilde{\alpha}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
by linear interpolation. It is clear that
\widetilde{\alpha}(x)\geq\alpha(x)
for all
x\geq 0
. Moreover, we let
\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t):=\widetilde{\alpha}(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t))
and
q(t):=C_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t)+\mathcal{K}(t))/t
, where
C_{1}>2(\zeta(\theta-d^{*})(1-\varepsilon))^{-1}
is a constant. We note that
\lim_{t\to\infty}q(t)=0
since
d
converges to
d^{*}
and
b
tends to infinity. We then have the following two cases:

(6.40)

(a)\quad i<\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{\mathcal{K}(t)}q(t),\qquad(b)\quad\frac{(1-\varepsilon)t}{\mathcal{K}(t)}q(t)\leq i\leq M.

Case (a).
As
\theta\in(d^{*},R)
, we use that
x_{i}\geq 0
and that
\mathcal{K}
is increasing to obtain the upper bound

(6.41)

\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t))+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)-(\theta-d^{*})x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)+\zeta d^{*}\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

By the bound on
i
, it follows that
x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)=o(t)
. Hence, by
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
we have
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t))=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)+o(x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t))
, where we use that
x_{i+1}=x_{i}+\zeta
in the final step. As a result, we can rewrite the upper bound to obtain

(6.42)

\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)-(\theta-d^{*}+o(1))x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)+\zeta d^{*}\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

Finally, as
x_{i}
grows linearly in
i
, we have for some large constant
C_{2}>0
,

(6.43)

\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor(1-\varepsilon)tq(t)/\mathcal{K}(t)\rfloor}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{i+1},S_{k+i}>t\right)\leq C_{2}\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+\zeta d^{*}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

Case (b).
With
\theta\in(d^{*},R)
, since
-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t))\leq\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t)
, and as
x_{i}\geq 0
,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\exp{}

\displaystyle\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t))+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t-x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t))-(\theta-d^{*})x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t)+\zeta d^{*}\mathcal{K}(t)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-d^{*}t+\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t)+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}-(\theta-d^{*})x_{i}+\zeta d^{*}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)\big)

As a result, since
x_{i}=i\zeta
increases linearly in
i
,

(6.45)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\lceil(1-\varepsilon)tq(t)/\mathcal{K}(t)\rceil}^{M-1}{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{i+1},S_{k_{i}}>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\exp\big(-d^{*}t+\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t)-(\theta-d^{*})\zeta(1-\varepsilon)q(t)t+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+\zeta d^{*}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

We now use the specific choice of the function
q
. For any
\theta\in(d^{*},R)
and since
\mathcal{K}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(t)\geq\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)
and
C_{1}>2(\zeta(\theta-d^{*})(1-\varepsilon))^{-1}
, we thus arrive at

(6.46)

\sum_{i=\lceil(1-\varepsilon)tq(t)/\mathcal{K}(t)\rceil}^{M-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k_{i+1},S_{k_{i}}>t\right)\leq\exp\big(-d^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)+\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+\zeta d^{*}-2+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t)\big).

We combine this with the bound in Case
(a)
and the bounds for first and second term in (
6.32
) and (
6.36
), respectively. We observe that the main contribution of these four bounds comes from Case
(a)
. Using all four bounds in (
6.30
), we finally obtain

(6.47)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log(\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right))-d^{*}+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)}{\mathcal{K}(t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(\theta^{2}-(d^{*})^{2}\big)+\zeta d^{*}.

The upper bound can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
\zeta
close to zero and
\theta
close to
d^{*}
. Together with the lower bound in (
6.28
), we arrive at the desired result.
∎

6.2.
Growth-rate of the branching process

In this section we provide some results for the growth-rate of the branching process
\mathrm{BP}
. We require a precise understanding of the growth-rate of the branching process to be able to analyse the quantities
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
and
I_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
in later Sections. We introduce the following notation. First, recall that
\mathcal{S}
denotes the event that the branching process survives and that

(6.48)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\cdot\right):=\mathbb{P}\!\left(\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{S}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{S}}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}\left[\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{S}\right]

denote the conditional probability measure and its corresponding expected value, under the event
\mathcal{S}
. For
0\leq s<t<\infty
, we further define

(6.49)

\mathcal{B}(s,t):=\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{\infty}:\sigma_{u}\in[s,t]\}

as the set of individuals born in the time interval
[s,t]
. Also, recall
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
from (
3.4
) as the set of individuals alive at time
t
, and
\mathcal{R}
from (
2.4
) as the point process that governs the production of offspring of individuals. We have the following results regarding the growth-rate of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
and
\mathcal{B}(s,t)
. The first is similar to the well-known Kesten-Stigum theorem and is a direct consequence of results for general CMJ branching processes in
[
19
,
33
]
; the second uses a similar approach as the proof of
[
2
, Lemma
7.12
]
.

Proposition 6.5

(Growth rate of branching process conditionally on survival)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied, and recall
\mathcal{S}
from (
3.8
) as the event that the process
\mathrm{BP}
survives. There exists a non-negative random variable
W
, such that

(6.50)

\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}|\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}W.

Moreover, with

(6.51)

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\lambda^{*}}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}u}\,\mathcal{R}(\mathrm{d}u),

the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\lambda^{*}}(\infty)\log^{+}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\lambda^{*}}(\infty)\right]<\infty
,

(b)

\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]>0
,

(c)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}|\right]\to\mathbb{E}\left[W\right]
as
t\to\infty
,

(d)

W>0
almost surely on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proposition
6.5
is a direct result of
[
33
, Theorem
5.4
]
and
[
19
]
(see also
[
33
, Proposition
1.1
]
for a condensed version). We have the following useful corollary.

Corollary 6.6
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied, and recall the event
\mathcal{S}
that the process
\mathrm{BP}
survives from (
3.8
). Let
r,s,u=r(t),s(t),u(t)\geq 0
be such that
s(t)-r(t)\to\infty
and
u(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
. Then,

(6.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\!s-u}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

and

(6.53)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|\geq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\!s+u}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Finally,

(6.54)

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\geq M\right)

\displaystyle=0,

and

(6.55)

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\inf_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\leq 1/M\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

We observe that the number of births between time
r
and time
s
equals the number of alive individuals at time
s
minus the number of individuals alive at time
r
plus the number of individuals born between time
r
and
s
that have died before time
s
. That is,

(6.56)

|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|=|\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}|-|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|+|\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{\infty}:\sigma_{u}\in[r,s],L_{u}<s-\sigma_{u}\}|\geq|\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}|-|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|.

Consequentially,

(6.57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\!s-u}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}|-|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\!s-u}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!s}-|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!r}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}(s-r)}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-u}\right).

By (
6.50
), it follows that
|\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!s}
converges almost surely to
W
, which is
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
-almost surely positive. Similarly,
|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!r}
is a tight sequence of random variables. Hence, since
s-r
tends to infinity with
t
, it follows that
|\mathcal{A}_{r}^{\mathrm{cont}}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!r}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}(s-r)}
converges to zero
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
-almost surely. As
u
tends to infinity with
t
, the right-hand side of the event in the probability tends to zero with
t
and we arrive at (
6.52
).

In a similar manner, we observe that
|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|\leq|\mathcal{B}(0,s)|
. It follows from
[
33
, Theorem
5.4
]
that
|\mathcal{B}(0,s)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!s}
converges almost surely (or, equivalently, from (
3.13
), since
|\mathcal{B}(0,s)|=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{s}
and
|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{s}|=Z^{\mathrm{a}}_{s}
), so that

(6.58)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(r,s)|\geq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\!s+u}\right)\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,s)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!s}\geq\mathrm{e}^{u}\right)=0,

since
u
tends to infinity with
t
.

Finally, we prove (
6.54
) and (
6.55
). Fix
\varepsilon>0
. First, by a union bound,

(6.59)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\geq M\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\in[0,\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})]}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\geq M\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\geq\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\geq M\right),

where we bound the exponential term in the first probability on the right-hand side from above by one. As
|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|
is increasing in
t
, we can bound this term from above further by

(6.60)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*}))|\geq M\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*}))|\geq\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda^{*}\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})}\right).

It thus follows from (
6.53
) (with
r=0
,
s=\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})
, and
u=\log(M)/2
) that the right-hand side is at most
\varepsilon/3
for all
M
large. For the second probability, we first take
M_{0}=M_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
large enough so that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq M_{0}\right)<\varepsilon/2
. Then, we take
M\geq M_{0}
large such that

(6.61)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\geq\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\geq M\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\geq\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\geq M_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(W\geq M_{0}\right)+\varepsilon/3<2\varepsilon/3.

Here, the second step follows from the fact that
\inf_{t\geq\log(M)/(2\lambda^{*})}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}W
as
M
tends to infinity. As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, this yields the desired result and proves (
6.54
).

The proof of (
6.55
) uses a similar approach, where we now use that
\mathcal{B}(0,t)\geq 1
for all
t\geq 0
, so that
|\mathcal{B}(0,s)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!s}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}T}
for all
s\leq T
. As a result, with
T=\log(M)/\lambda^{*}
,

(6.62)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\inf_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\leq 1/M\right)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\inf_{t\geq\log(M)/\lambda^{*}}\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\leq 1/M\right).

Then, fix
\varepsilon>0
and take
M_{0}=M_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
large so that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(W\leq 1/M_{0}\right)\leq\varepsilon/2
. We note that this is possible, conditionally on survival, since then
W>0
almost surely by
(d)
. Now, take
M\geq M_{0}
large so that

(6.63)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\inf_{t\geq\log(M)/\lambda^{*}}\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\leq 1/M\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\inf_{t\geq\log(M)/\lambda^{*}}\!\!\!\!\!|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\leq 1/M_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(W\leq 1/M_{0}\right)+\varepsilon/2\leq\varepsilon.

Here, the second step follows from the fact that
\inf_{t\geq\log(M)/\lambda^{*}}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}-\mathrm{a.s.}}W
as
M
to infinity. As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, this proves (
6.55
) and concludes the proof.
∎

6.3.
Non-survival of old individuals

We conclude this section by combining the results from the previous sub-sections to prove asymptotic results for
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
. In essence, we show that all individuals that are ‘too old’ have all died by time
t
with high probability and that there exists ‘young enough’ individuals that survive up to time
t
. To formalise this intuition, we introduce the following notation. Fix
d^{*}\geq 0,\lambda^{*}>0
, and
p>0
, and define

(6.64)

F_{p,t}:=\big[t-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big),t+p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big].

We observe that the behaviour of
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
is different in the ‘rich are old’ and the ‘rich die young’ regimes, since the lifetime of an individual has a different (asymptotic) distribution in these regimes. This is made precise in the following result.

Proposition 6.7
.

Suppose that the sequences
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
), (
F-D
), and (
Ma
) are satisfied. Recall
R
from (
2.13
) and the event
\mathcal{S}
from (
3.8
) that the branching process
\mathrm{BP}
survives. Assume that
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)>R
. Then, there exists
K_{0}>0
such that for any
K>K_{0}
there exists
p>0
sufficiently small so that

(6.65)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in[t-p\log t,t+p\log t]:\Big|O^{\mathrm{cont}}_{s}-\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t\Big|\leq K\log t\right)=1.

Now, assume that
b
diverges and that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,R)
. Also, suppose that Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and Assumption (
D-V
) are satisfied. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p>0
such that

(6.66)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in F_{p,t}:\Big|\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)^{-1}\big(O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\tfrac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)\Big|<\varepsilon\right)=1.

Finally, if Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied,

(6.67)

O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}-\mathrm{a.s.}}O^{\prime},

for some almost surely finite random variable
O^{\prime}
.

Remark 6.8
.

Under the assumption that
\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)\geq R
we can adapt the proof of (
6.65
) (and by using (
6.1
) rather than (
6.2
)) to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p>0
such that

(6.68)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]:\Big|\frac{O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}}{t}-\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\Big|<\varepsilon\right)=1.

It yields a weaker bound on
O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}
, but with a weaker condition on
d
and a larger range of
s
.

\blacktriangleleft

Remark 6.9
.

When setting
s=t
in (
6.65
) and (
6.66
), the quantity in the absolute values converges to zero in probability. Though in some cases where
\mathcal{K}
grows sufficiently fast it may be possible to strengthen the convergence in probability to almost sure convergence, we are unable to derive the stronger bounds due to the use of (
6.52
) and (
6.53
) in Corollary
6.6
. Stronger versions of these results would be required to improve to almost sure convergence.
\blacktriangleleft

Remark 6.10
.

Proposition
6.7
provides the main step in proving the asymptotic behaviour of
O_{n}
, as presented in Theorems
2.3
through
2.10
. It remains to translate the asymptotic behaviour of
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
into the asymptotic behaviour of
O_{n}
, which is carried out in Section
9
.

\blacktriangleleft

Intuitively, an individual born at time
T<t
survives up to time
t
with probability
\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t-T\right)
, independently of all other individuals. At the same time, the number of individuals born around time
T
is roughly
\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}T}
by Proposition
6.5
. The proof uses first and second moment bounds to establish the optimal choice of
T
such that no individuals born before
T
are alive at time
t
, whereas many individuals born after
T
are alive at time
t
.

Proof.

We first prove (
6.65
). Fix
K,p>0
and define

(6.69)

\ell_{t}:=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t,\qquad u_{t}:=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t,\qquad\widetilde{u}_{t}:=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t,

and

(6.70)

\underline{r}(t):=t-p\log t,\qquad\overline{r}(t):=t+p\log t.

We then observe that
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
is increasing in
t
, so that

(6.71)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\Big|O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t\Big|<K\log t\text{ for all }s\in[\underline{r}(t),\overline{r}(t)]\bigg)\geq 1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\underline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\leq\ell_{t}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\overline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq u_{t}\right).

We thus aim to bound the probabilities on the right-hand side from above, starting with the leftmost one. For any
\delta>0
, recalling that
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
denotes the set of alive individuals at time
t
,

(6.72)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\underline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\leq\ell_{t}\right)={}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\underline{r}(t)}\neq\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\Big[\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\underline{r}(t)}\neq\varnothing,|\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}+\delta\log t}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})|\geq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}+\delta\log t}\right)\Big].

The second probability in the brackets converges to zero by Corollary
6.6
(with
r=0,s=\ell_{t}
, and
u=\delta\log t
). Furthermore, lifetimes among individuals are i.i.d. and each individual born before time
\ell_{t}
needs to live for at least
\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t}
time to be alive at time
\underline{r}(t)
. Hence, by a union bound and using the upper bound in (
6.2
) of Lemma
6.1
, we arrive for some large constant
C>0
at the upper bound

(6.73)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}+\delta\log t}\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t}\right)+o(1)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}+\delta\log t-R(\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t})+C\log(\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t})}+o(1).

By the definition of
\ell_{t}
and
\underline{r}(t)
, this equals

(6.74)

\exp\Big(\big[-(\lambda^{*}+R)K+\delta+Rp+C\big]\log t+\mathcal{O}(1)\Big)+o(1).

Hence, for any
K
larger than
K_{0}:=C(\lambda^{*}+R)^{-1}
, we can choose
\delta
and
p
sufficiently small so that the terms in the square brackets are negative. As a result, the upper bound in (
6.73
) tends to zero with
t
for these choices of
K,p
, and
\delta
.

In a similar way, we bound the second probability on the right-hand side of (
6.71
). We recall
\widetilde{u}_{t}
from (
6.69
) and write

(6.75)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(O_{\overline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq u_{t})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\overline{r}(t)}=\varnothing)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\forall v\in\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t}):L^{(v)}\leq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\big).

For ease of writing, we define the events

(6.76)

\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\{\forall v\in\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t}):L^{(v)}\leq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{t}:=\{|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|>m\}\quad\text{for }m\in\mathbb{N},t\geq 0.

We then write the right-hand side of (
6.75
) as

(6.77)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\right]+\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}^{c}}\right].

The second term on the right-hand side equals the probability of the event
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{c}
under the conditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
. For the first term, we condition on two things. First, we condition
\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{u}_{t}}
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the branching process up to time
\widetilde{u}_{t}
. Second, we condition on
|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|
, the number of individuals born in the branching process in the interval
(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})
. We can thus write

(6.78)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{u}_{t}},|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{u}_{t}},|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{u}_{t}},|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\right)\right].

Conditionally on
|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|
and upon the event
\mathcal{D}_{t}
, we can use the independence of the lifetimes of distinct individuals to bound the terms in the expected value from above by

(6.79)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{u}_{t}},|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\right)\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\big(1-\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\right)\big)^{m}\leq\exp\big(-m\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\right)\big),

where we have omitted the indicator random variable and used that
1-x\leq\mathrm{e}^{-x}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
in the last step. Combining all of the above in (
6.75
), we thus arrive at

(6.80)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\overline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq u_{t}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp\big(-m\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\right)\big)+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\leq m\right).

We choose
m=\exp(\lambda^{*}u_{t}-\delta\log t)
and apply Corollary
6.6
(with
r=\widetilde{u}_{t}
,
s=u_{t}
, and
u=\delta\log t
) to the second term on the right-hand side. This yields that the second term tends to zero with
t
. For the first term we arrive at

(6.81)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp\big(-\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}u_{t}-\delta\log t}\big).

By the lower bound in (
6.2
) of Lemma
6.1
and the definition of
u_{t}
and
\widetilde{u}_{t}
, we arrive at

(6.82)

\exp\Big(-(\exp\Big(\big[\lambda^{*}K-\delta-Rp\big]\log t+\mathcal{O}(1)\Big)\Big).

For any
K>0
(and thus for any
K>K_{0}
, in particular) we can choose
\delta
and
p
sufficiently small so that the upper bound tends to zero. This yields that the left-hand side of (
6.71
) converges to one as
t
tends to infinity, so that the desired result follows.

We then prove (
6.66
). The proof follows a similar argument as the above, but with a refined result. To this end, we use Lemma
6.3
and Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
. From the latter we obtain
r=r(t)
such that

(6.83)

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)=o(\mathcal{K}(r(t))).

We fix
\varepsilon>0
and set

(6.84)

\displaystyle\ell_{t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(r(t)),

\displaystyle u_{t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(r(t)),

\displaystyle\underline{r}(t){}

\displaystyle:=t-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big),

\displaystyle\overline{r}(t)

\displaystyle:=t+p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big).

We then use a similar bound as in (
6.71
), to obtain

(6.85)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Big({}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)^{-1}\big(O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\tfrac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)\Big|<\varepsilon,\text{ for all }s\in[\underline{r}(t),\overline{r}(t)]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\underline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\leq\ell_{t}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\overline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq u_{t}\right).

We again bound the probabilities on the right-hand side from above, and start with the leftmost one. We use a similar bound as in (
6.72
), but with the event

(6.86)

\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\big\{|\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})|\leq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}+\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))\big)\big\},

where
\delta>0
is small. We then have the upper bound

(6.87)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\underline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\leq\ell_{t}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\underline{r}(t)}\neq\varnothing\right)\leq\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\big[\mathbb{P}\!\left(\{\mathcal{B}(0,\ell_{t})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\underline{r}(t)}\neq\varnothing\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}\right)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}\right)\big].

Again, by Corollary
6.6
(with
r=0
,
s=\ell_{t}
, and
u=\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))
) and as
\mathcal{K}
and
s
diverge, the term
\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}\right)
tends to zero with
t
. We can thus focus on the first term in the square brackets. With a similar approach as in (
6.73
) and with
\xi>0
fixed, but using Lemma
6.3
instead, we bound this term from above by

(6.88)

\displaystyle\exp\big(\lambda^{*}\ell_{t}{}

\displaystyle+\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))-d^{*}(\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t})-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t})+\xi\mathcal{K}(\underline{r}(t)-\ell_{t})\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big({}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(r(t))-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)

\displaystyle+(\delta-(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})\varepsilon)\mathcal{K}(r(t))+\xi\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(r(t))-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)

\displaystyle+d^{*}p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\Big).

We combine (
6.83
) with
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
to obtain that
r(t)=\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+o(t)
. Further, as
b
diverges, it follows that
\mathcal{K}(t)=o(t)
by
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
. We then apply Assumption
\mathcal{K}
to write

(6.89)

\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(r(t))-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)=(1+o(1))\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big),

and

(6.90)

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)=o\big(\mathcal{K}(r(t))\big)=o\big(\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big).

We thus arrive at

(6.91)

\exp\Big((\delta+\xi+d^{*}p-(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})\varepsilon+o(1))\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\Big).

By choosing
\delta
,
p
, and
\xi
sufficiently small with respect to
\varepsilon
, the upper bound tends to zero with
t
.

We then bound the second probability on the right-hand side of (
6.85
). For ease of writing, we set

(6.92)

\widetilde{u}_{t}:=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)).

Then, using the event

(6.93)

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t}:=\big\{|\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t})|\geq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}u_{t}-\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))\big)\big\},

with
\delta>0
small, we follow the same steps as in (
6.75
) through (
6.81
) to arrive at

(6.94)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\overline{r}(t)}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq u_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{B}\big(\widetilde{u}_{t},u_{t}\big)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\overline{r}(t)}=\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp\big(-\mathbb{P}\!\left(L\geq\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t}\right)\exp\big(\lambda^{*}u_{t}-\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))\big)\big)+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t}^{c}\right).

Again, by Corollary
6.6
(with
r=\widetilde{u}_{t}
,
s=u_{t}
, and
u=\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))
), the final term tends to zero. By using Lemma
6.3
, we bound the argument of the exponential term from above, with
\xi>0
, by

(6.95)

\displaystyle-{}

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\lambda^{*}u_{t}-\delta\mathcal{K}(r(t))-d^{*}(\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t})-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t})-\xi\mathcal{K}(\overline{r}(t)-\widetilde{u}_{t})\Big)

\displaystyle=-\exp\Big(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)+(\varepsilon(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})-\delta)\mathcal{K}(r(t))

\displaystyle-\xi\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)-d^{*}p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\Big).

We then combine this with (
6.89
) and (
6.90
) in (
6.94
) to arrive at

(6.96)

\exp\Big(-\exp\big([\varepsilon(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})-\delta-\xi-d^{*}p+o(1)]\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\big)\big)\Big)+o(1),

which tends to zero with
t
when we choose
\delta
,
p
, and
\xi
sufficiently small with respect to
\varepsilon
, since
\mathcal{K}
tends to infinity. We thus obtain that the left-hand side of (
6.85
) tends to one as
t
tends to infinity, which yields the desired result.

Finally, we prove (
6.67
). It follows from Lemma
5.3
that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=\infty\right)>0
when Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied. Furthermore, Corollary
6.4
implies that
L
is finite almost surely, conditionally on
\{D<\infty\}
, and that
L
is infinite almost surely, conditionally on
\{D=\infty\}
. As a result, let
N
denote the number of individuals born in the branching process, when the first individual
v
, such that
D^{(v)}=\infty
, is born. From the independence of
(D^{(v)})_{v\in\mathcal{U}_{\infty}}
, it follows that
N
is geometrically distributed with parameter
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=\infty\right)>0
. Hence,
N
is finite almost surely. Then, let
v_{i}
be the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
individual born in the branching process with birth-time
\sigma_{i}:=\sigma_{v_{i}}
, for
i\in[N]
. Note that, since
\sigma_{i}\neq\sigma_{j}
almost surely and there is a finite number of births in any neighbourhood of
0
almost surely, this enumeration is well-defined. Moreover,
L_{v_{i}}<\infty
almost surely for all
i\in[N-1]
and
L_{v_{N}}=\infty
, by definition. It thus follows that, conditionally on
\mathcal{S}
,

(6.97)

O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}=\sigma_{N}\quad\text{for all }t\geq\max_{i\in[N-1]}(\sigma_{i}+L_{v_{i}}).

Indeed, the event
\mathcal{S}
ensures that the individual
v_{N}
is born before all other individuals
v_{i}
, with
i<N
, die. Note that the birth of an individual with an infinite lifetime then guarantees survival of the branching process. Further, once all individuals
v_{1},\ldots,v_{N-1}
have died,
v_{N}
is the oldest alive individual and, as it lives forever, remains to be so for all time after. This yields (
6.67
) with
O^{\prime}=\tau_{N}
and thus completes the proof.
∎

7.
The individual with the largest offspring in the ‘rich are old’ regime

In this section we analyse the growth-rate of
I_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
in the ‘rich are old’ regime. Recall
R
from (
2.13
), and
\varphi_{2}
from (
2.8
). We assume that
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity and that either Assumption (
F-D
) is not met, or otherwise that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}
for some
d^{*}\in[0,\infty)
. Finally, we recall the set
F_{p,t}
from (
6.64
). We then have the following result.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that
b
tends to infinity and such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied. Further, suppose that one of the following holds:

(1)

Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied. We set
d^{*}:=0
.

(2)

Assumption (
F-D
) is satisfied,
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=d^{*}\in[0,\infty)
, and Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
is satisfied.

Finally, suppose that Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and (
D-V
) are satisfied. Then, conditionally on
\mathcal{S}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exists
p>0
such that the following event holds with high probability as
t
tends to infinity: For all
s\in F_{p,t}
,

(7.1)

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t)}\Big(I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\Big)-\frac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}\bigg|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon,

and

(7.2)

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t)}\Big(\varphi_{1}(\max_{v\in\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}}\deg^{(v)}(s))-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\Big)-\frac{\lambda^{*}-d^{*}}{2}\bigg|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon.

Remark 7.2
.

When setting
s=t
in the above result, we obtain convergence to zero of the quantities in the absolute values. Similar to Remark
6.9
, it may be possible to strengthen this to almost sure convergence if
\mathcal{K}
grows sufficiently fast. However, this again requires stronger results compared to those in Corollary
6.6
.
\blacktriangleleft

Remark 7.3
.

Proposition
7.1
also provides a precise asymptotic expansion for the birth-time of the individual with the largest offspring when
d^{*}\geq R
, i.e. in the
rich die young regime
. However, we prove a (weaker) lower bound for
I^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
in this regime that holds in greater generality in Section
8
.
\blacktriangleleft

Remark 7.4
.

In Case
(1)
Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied, i.e. when
\rho_{1}
converges. By definition
\alpha\equiv\rho_{1}
in this case. As a result,
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
converges in this case. Since
\mathcal{K}
diverges by Assumption (
D-V
), the results in Proposition
7.1
clearly hold when one omits the term
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))
. Similarly and related to Remark
2.7
, when Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied but
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(i)=0
such that
\alpha=\rho_{1}=o(\varphi_{2})
, the condition that Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
is satisfied can be omitted.
\blacktriangleleft

We split the proof of Proposition
7.1
into several parts. Let us first outline the proof strategy.

We define the functions
H,\widetilde{H}\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
as

(7.3)

H(x):=-(x+d^{*})+\sqrt{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})x}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{H}(x):=\begin{cases}H(x)&\mbox{if }x\geq\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})},\\
-\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}&\mbox{if }x<\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}.\end{cases}

It is readily checked that
H
and
\widetilde{H}
have a unique maximum at
u^{*}>0
, with

(7.4)

u^{*}:=\frac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\ H(u^{*})=\widetilde{H}(u^{*})=\frac{\lambda^{*}-d^{*}}{2}.

See also Figure
3
.

H(x)
\widetilde{H}(x)

.

and

, with their unique maximum

.

We then partition the time interval
[0,t]
into a variety of intervals. Recall the functions
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{K}
, defined in (
2.10
). First, for
\varepsilon>0
small, we omit the interval

(7.5)

\Big[0,\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\varepsilon\mathcal{K}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\Big)\Big],

since we know by Proposition
6.7
that no individual born in this interval is alive at time
s
, for any
s\in F_{p,t}
with high probability. Then, we consider

(7.6)

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{e}}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\varepsilon\mathcal{K}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\Big),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\Big],

and, for some large integer
A\in\mathbb{N}
to be determined,

(7.7)

\displaystyle U^{\ell}_{t}(A)

\displaystyle:=\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+A\mathcal{K}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\Big),t\Big]

as two intervals where individuals with ‘too few’ offspring are born. We further partition these intervals into smaller parts later. What remains is the interval

(7.8)

\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+A\mathcal{K}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t\Big)\Big].

We partition
[0,u^{*}-\varepsilon)\cup[u^{*}+\varepsilon,A)
into intervals
[x_{i},x_{i+1})
of length
\zeta<u^{*}-\varepsilon
for
i\in\mathbb{I}
(with
\mathbb{I}
an index set depending on
A
and
\varepsilon
), and let
t^{*}:=\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t
for ease of writing. Then, we partition the interval in (
7.8
) into

(7.9)

\widetilde{U}_{t,i}:=\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)\quad\text{for }i\in\mathbb{I},

and

(7.10)

U_{t}:=\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(u^{*}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(u^{*}+\varepsilon)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big),

see Figure
4
.

.
Partition of the interval
[0,t]
. The red interval is the part we omit. The smallest intervals are the

with

.

The aim of the proof is to show that the probability that there exists an individual who, at time
s
, is alive, has the largest offspring, and has a birth-time in
U_{t}
, for all
s\in F_{p,t}
, converges to one, for some sufficiently small
p>0
(with
F_{p,t}
as in (
6.64
)). To make this precise, we let

•

D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)
denote the largest offspring of individuals born in
U_{t}
and alive at time
s
,

•

D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)
denote the largest offspring among individuals born in
\widetilde{U}_{t,i}
and alive at time
s
,

•

D^{\max}_{\varepsilon,t}(s)
denote the largest offspring among individuals born in
U^{\mathrm{e}}_{t}
and alive at time
s
,

•

D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)
denote the largest offspring among individuals born in
U^{\ell}_{t}(A)
and alive at time
s
.

Then, we take

(7.11)

\xi=\xi(\varepsilon):=\frac{1}{2}\min\{H(u^{*})-H(u^{*}-\varepsilon),H(u^{*})-H(u^{*}+\varepsilon)\}=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}-\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3}).

As a result, we have
H(u^{*})-\xi>\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
when
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, since
-\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}<\frac{\lambda^{*}-d^{*}}{2}=H(u^{*})
. As a result,

(7.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}{}

\displaystyle\in U_{t}\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big({}

\displaystyle D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\geq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,

\displaystyle D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{I},

\displaystyle D^{\max}_{\varepsilon,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(d^{*}-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,

\displaystyle D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)\rceil,\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big).

Indeed, the event in the lower bound guarantees that
I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in U_{t}
for all
s\in F_{p,t}
, since
H
has a unique maximum at
u^{*}
and
\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi<H(u^{*})-\xi
. A union bound thus yields that

(7.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle(\{I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in U_{t}\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\{D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\geq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\}^{c})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\{D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\{D^{\max}_{\varepsilon,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(d^{*}-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\{D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\}^{c}\big).

We show in the next three lemmas that each term on the right-hand side converges to zero as
t
tends to infinity. As
\mathbb{I}
is a finite set, this proves the first result in Proposition
7.1
.

Lemma 7.5

(Lower bound on offspring of individuals in the the optimal window)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
satisfy the conditions in Proposition
7.1
and recall
\xi
from (
7.11
). There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exist
p=p(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(7.14)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\geq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t})=1.

The lemma shows that there exist individuals born in the ‘optimal window’
U_{t}
that are alive and have a large offspring around time
t
. The next lemma provides upper bound for the size of the offspring of individuals born close to the optimal window (i.e. in a sub-optimal window), as well as for individuals in the optimal window.

Lemma 7.6

(Upper bound on offspring of individuals in the (sub-)optimal window)
.

Suppose
b
and
d
satisfy the conditions in Proposition
7.1
and recall
\xi
from (
7.11
). There exist
\zeta=\zeta(\varepsilon),p=p(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
,

(7.15)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big)=1.

Similarly, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exists
p=p(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(7.16)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t})=1,

Finally, exists
p(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(7.17)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{\varepsilon,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(d^{*}-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big)=1.

Remark 7.7
.

The first result in Lemma
7.6
combined with (
7.13
) implies the second result of Proposition
7.1
.
\blacktriangleleft

Finally, the third lemma shows that individuals that are born far away from the optimal window have small offspring compared to those in the optimal window.

Lemma 7.8

(Individuals far outside the optimal window have few offspring)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
satisfy the conditions in Proposition
7.1
. There exist
A>0
large and
p>0
small such that

(7.18)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big)\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big)=1.

It is clear that Lemmas
7.5
,
7.6
, and
7.8
, combined with Proposition
6.7
, imply Proposition
7.1
. Furthermore, Proposition
7.1
is the main part for the proof of (
2.16
) in Theorem
2.3
and (
2.18
) and (
2.19
) in Theorem
2.5
.

We proceed by proving the three lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

For ease of writing, we set

(7.19)

k:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,

and for some small constant
c\in(0,1)
to be determined, we let
\mathcal{B}(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2},u^{*})
denote the set of individuals born in the interval

(7.20)

\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2})\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big],

which we note is a subset of the individuals whose offspring we take the maximum of in the random variable
D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)
. We can thus bound

(7.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\big(D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\geq k,\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\exists v\in\mathcal{B}(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2},u^{*}):v\text{ alive at time }t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\deg^{(v)}(t-p\mathcal{K}(t^{*}))\geq k\right).

Indeed, restring ourselves to individuals born in
\mathcal{B}(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2},u^{*})
and requiring that individuals live until the end of the interval
F_{p,t}
and produce at least
k
offspring by the start of the interval yields a lower bound. We first write
\{v\text{ is alive at time }t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\text{ and }\deg^{(v)}(t-p\mathcal{K}(t^{*}))\geq k\}
as

(7.22)

\{D^{(v)}\geq k,S^{(v)}_{k}\leq t-p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-\sigma_{v},S^{(v)}_{D^{(v)}+1}>t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-\sigma_{v}\}.

By using that
v\in\mathcal{B}(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2},u^{*})
, we can bound its birth-time
\sigma_{v}
from below and above in the second and third part of the event, respectively. Hence, the desired event is a subset of

(7.23)

\displaystyle\{{}

\displaystyle D^{(v)}\geq k,S^{(v)}_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),

\displaystyle S^{(v)}_{D^{(v)}+1}>t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\}.

With a similar approach as in the steps between (
6.75
) and (
6.81
) and by leveraging the independence of the reproduction process of distinct individuals (when not conditioning on survival), we obtain for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
the lower bound

(7.24)

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(|\mathcal{B}(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2},u^{*}{}

\displaystyle)|\leq m)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\Big[1-\mathbb{P}\Big({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),

\displaystyle S_{D+1}>t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)\Big]^{m}.

We then set

(7.25)

m:=\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\lambda^{*}-\delta)u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big),

and conclude that the probability on the first line tends to zero with
t
for any
\delta>0
by Corollary
6.6
(with
r=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(u^{*}-\varepsilon)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
,
s=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
, and
u=\delta u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
) and since
\mathcal{K}
diverges. What remains, is to bound the term in the straight brackets in (
7.24
) from above. By using that
1-x\leq\mathrm{e}^{-x}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain the upper bound

(7.26)

\displaystyle\exp\bigg({}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\Big(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),

\displaystyle S_{D+1}>t^{*}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\lambda^{*}-\delta)u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)\bigg).

We bound the probability from below to obtain an upper bound. From this point onwards, we consider Case
(2)
in the statement of Proposition
7.1
. We comment on the required adaptations in Case
(1)
at the end of the proof.

In Case
(2)
and for any
\eta>0
, there exists
t^{\prime}>0
such that, for all
t\geq t^{\prime}
, we have
d(i)\leq d^{*}+\eta
for all
i\geq k
(as
k
grows with
t
). We can thus use
(ii)
in Lemma
5.9
to obtain the lower bound

(7.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathrm{e}^{-(d^{*}+\eta)(2p+c\varepsilon^{2})\mathcal{K}(t^{*})}\mathbb{E}\bigg[{}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\{S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\}

\displaystyle\exp\Big((d^{*}+\eta)\big(S_{k}-\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\big)\Big)\bigg].

By the choice of
k
, it follows that

(7.28)

\varphi_{1}(k-1)\leq t^{*}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(H(u^{*})-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\leq\varphi_{1}(k).

Hence, as
\mathcal{K}(t^{*})=(1+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by Assumption
\mathcal{K}
and
(a)
and
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
, and
\varphi(k-1)=\varphi_{1}(k)-o(1)
as
b
diverges,

(7.29)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(k)-(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)

\displaystyle\leq t^{*}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}p-\xi)\varphi_{2}(k),

for all
t
large. We thus obtain the lower bound

(7.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathrm{e}^{-(d^{*}+\eta)(2p+c\varepsilon^{2})\mathcal{K}(t^{*})}\mathbb{E}\bigg[{}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\{S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)\}

\displaystyle\exp\Big((d^{*}+\eta)\big(S_{k}-\big(\varphi_{1}(k)-(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}p-\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)\big)\big)\Big)\bigg].

Note that
H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi=\lambda^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi>0
for
\xi
sufficiently small. As a result, we can use Proposition
5.12
, with
z=H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi>0
,
y=H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}p-\xi
, and
\theta=d^{*}+\eta
, to bound the expected value from below by

(7.31)

\exp\big(-\big[(1+(d^{*}+\eta))p+\tfrac{1}{2}(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)^{2}\big]\varphi_{2}(k)\big).

We combine this with Lemma
5.4
