. By Assumption (
F-D
),
\rho_{1}
diverges. If, furthermore,
\rho_{2}
also diverges, then

(7.32)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-(\frac{1}{2}+o(1))\rho_{2}(k)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-(\frac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t^{*})},

where we combine that
d
converges to
d^{*}\in[0,\infty)
and thus
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by
(c)
in Lemma
5.13
together with Assumption
\mathcal{K}
in the last step. If, on the other hand,
\rho_{2}
converges (which can only occur for
d^{*}=0
), then again by Lemma
5.4
,

(7.33)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-\mathcal{O}(1)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-o(\mathcal{K}(t^{*}))},

where the final step follows since
\varphi_{2}
diverges by Assumption (
D-V
). We can unify both cases by writing
o(\mathcal{K}(t^{*}))=(\frac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
in the second case, for which
d^{*}=0
. Together with (
7.31
), this yields the lower bound

(7.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),

\displaystyle S_{D+1}>t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}-c\varepsilon^{2}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\rho_{1}(k)-(\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-(d^{*}+\eta)(2p+c\varepsilon^{2})\mathcal{K}(t^{*})

\displaystyle-\big[(1+(d^{*}+\eta))p+\tfrac{1}{2}(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)^{2}\big]\varphi_{2}(k)\Big).

Writing
\rho_{1}(k)=d^{*}\varphi_{1}(k)+\alpha(k)
and applying Assumption
\mathcal{K}
to the term
\varphi_{2}(k)
together with
(a)
and
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
, we arrive at

(7.35)

\exp\Big(-d^{*}\varphi_{1}(k)-\alpha(k)-\big[\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)^{2}+p+(3p+c\varepsilon^{2})(d^{*}+\eta)+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

Finally, by the choice of
k
and by using Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
, we obtain

(7.36)

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\big[{}

\displaystyle d^{*}(H(u^{*})-\xi)+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)^{2}+p

\displaystyle+(3p+c\varepsilon^{2})(d^{*}+\eta)+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

Multiplying this with the exponential term on the second line of (
7.26
) and using that
H(u^{*})+u^{*}=\lambda^{*}
, we bound the inner exponential in (
7.26
) from below

(7.37)

\displaystyle\exp\Big({}

\displaystyle-\big[-(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})u^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}(H(u^{*})+u^{*}+d^{*})^{2}\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})+\xi\big[H(u^{*})+u^{*}+d^{*}\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})

\displaystyle-\big[\tfrac{3}{2}p\lambda^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\tfrac{3}{2}p-\xi)^{2}+p+(3p+c\varepsilon^{2})(d^{*}+\eta)+\delta u^{*}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

The terms in the first brackets equal zero by the choice of
H
and
u^{*}
, and the terms in the second brackets equal
\lambda^{*}+d^{*}>0
. Then, we recall that
\xi
is of the order
\varepsilon^{2}
, as follows from (
7.11
). Hence, by choosing
c
sufficiently small, that is,
c<\frac{1}{4}(d^{*}+\eta)^{-1}
, it follows that
(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})\xi-c(d^{*}+\eta)\varepsilon^{2}
is strictly positive when
\varepsilon
and
\eta
are sufficiently small. The remaining terms on the second line can be made arbitrarily small with respect to
\varepsilon^{2}
by choosing
p,\eta
, and
\delta
sufficiently small. As a result, the entire term diverges with
t
, so that we arrive at the desired result.

To deal with Case
(1)
, we apply
(iii)
in Lemma
5.9
to the probability in (
7.26
) to obtain the upper bound

(7.38)

\displaystyle\exp\bigg({}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\Big(S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(u^{*}+p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\lambda^{*}-\delta)u^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)\bigg).

The remaining steps in the proof above can then be followed, using Lemma
5.10
rather than Proposition
5.12
, to arrive at (
7.37
) with
d^{*}
set to equal
0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

We set

(7.39)

k:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,

and

(7.40)

B(x_{i},x_{i+1}):=\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

Then, we bound

(7.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\big(D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)\leq k,\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\exists v\in B(x_{i},x_{i+1})\ \exists s\in F_{p,t}:v\text{ alive at time }s\text{ and }\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k\big)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\exists v\in B(x_{i},x_{i+1})\ \exists s\in F_{p,t}:D\geq k,S_{k}\leq s-\sigma_{v},S_{D+1}>s-\sigma_{v}\right).

We bound the probability on the last line from above to show that it tends to zero with
t
. First, as
s\in F_{p,t}
and omitting the requirement that
S_{D+1}>s-\sigma_{v}
, we have the inclusion

(7.42)

\{D\geq k,S_{k}\leq s-\sigma_{v},S_{D+1}>s-\sigma_{v}\}\subseteq\{D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-\sigma_{v}\}.

We then use that
v\in B(x_{i},x_{i+1})
to bound
\sigma_{v}
from below. We also introduce the event

(7.43)

\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\Big\{|B(x_{i},x_{i+1})|\leq\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\lambda^{*}+\delta)x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)\Big)\Big\}.

By using a union bound, we then arrive at the upper bound

(7.44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(\exists v\in B(x_{i},x_{i+1}){}

\displaystyle\exists s\in F_{p,t}:D\geq k,S_{k}\leq s-\sigma_{v},S_{D+1}>s-\sigma_{v})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp{}

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\lambda^{*}+\delta)x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\Big({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(x_{i}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}\right).

The last term tends to zero by Corollary
6.6
(with
r=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
,
s=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
, and
u=\delta x_{i+1}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
), since the event
\mathcal{S}
has positive probability and because
\mathcal{K}
diverges by Assumption (
D-V
). By the choice of
k
, it follows that

(7.45)

\varphi_{1}(k)\geq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}).

Hence, as
\mathcal{K}(t^{*})=(1+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)
by Assumption
\mathcal{K}
and
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
,

(7.46)

\varphi_{1}(k)-(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i}-\tfrac{3}{2}p+\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)\geq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(x_{i}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),

for all
t
large. Using this in the first probability on the third line of (
7.44
) combined with the independence of
D
and
S_{k}
, we thus obtain the upper bound

(7.47)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(x_{i}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)\leq-(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i}-\tfrac{3}{2}p+\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)\right).

We now distinguish between two cases:
(a)

x_{i+1}\geq\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}
and
(b)

x_{i+1}<\frac{(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}
. In Case
(a)
, it is readily checked that
\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}\geq 0
. Hence, by choosing
p
and
\zeta
small such that
2p+\zeta<\xi
, noting that
x_{i}=x_{i+1}-\zeta
, it follows that
\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i}-\frac{3}{2}p+\xi>\frac{1}{2}p>0
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
. We can thus use Lemma
5.10
to bound

(7.48)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)\leq-(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i}-\tfrac{3}{2}p+\xi)\varphi_{2}(k)\right)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}-\zeta-2p+\xi)^{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\big).

In Case
(b)
, we instead bound the probability on the right-hand side from above by one. We then apply Lemma
5.4
to also bound
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\leq\exp\big(-\rho_{1}(k)-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)\big)
. As
\rho_{1}
is increasing,

(7.49)

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(k)\geq{}

\displaystyle\rho_{1}\big(\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle d^{*}t^{*}-\tfrac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+d^{*}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big),

where we use the definitions of
\alpha
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
as in (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), respectively, in the last step. Finally, we also note that
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
since
d
converges to
d^{*}
and by using
(c)
in Lemma
5.13
and Assumption
\mathcal{K}
. Using both bounds in (
7.47
) and Assumption
\mathcal{K}
, we arrive at

(7.50)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(D\geq k{}

\displaystyle,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(x_{i}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big({}

\displaystyle-d^{*}t^{*}+\tfrac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

\displaystyle-\big[d^{*}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)+\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}-\zeta-2p+\xi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

in Case
(a)
and

(7.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(D\geq k{}

\displaystyle,S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(x_{i}-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big({}

\displaystyle-d^{*}t^{*}+\tfrac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

\displaystyle-\big[d^{*}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

in Case
(b)
. We now make two further case distinctions according to the conditions in the statement of Proposition
7.1
. In Case
(2)
we apply
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
and Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
to obtain

(7.52)

\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+o(\mathcal{K}(t)).

As a result, we can simplify the upper bound in (
7.50
) to

(7.53)

\exp\big(-d^{*}t^{*}-\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\big[d^{*}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)+\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}-\zeta-2p+\xi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

and the one in (
7.51
) to

(7.54)

\exp\big(-d^{*}t^{*}-\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\big[d^{*}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+\xi)+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big).

These upper bounds remain valid in Case
(1)
, since we can change the
o(\mathcal{K}(t))
in (
7.52
) to
o(1)
(without the need for Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
), as
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)
converges with
t
in this case. This completes the upper bound for the first term on the second line of (
7.44
). Multiplying this with the exponential term on the first line of (
7.44
) yields

(7.55)

\displaystyle\exp\Big({}

\displaystyle\big[(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})x_{i+1}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}+d^{*})^{2}\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-(\xi-(\zeta+2p))\big[\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}+d^{*}\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})

\displaystyle-\big[\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi-(\zeta+2p))^{2}+(\zeta+2p)d^{*}-\delta x_{i+1}+o(1)\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

in Case
(a)
and

(7.56)

\displaystyle\exp{}

\displaystyle\big([\lambda^{*}x_{i+1}-d^{*}\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})-\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}-d^{*}\xi+\delta x_{i+1}+o(1)]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\big[\lambda^{*}x_{i+1}-\tfrac{\lambda^{*}(d^{*})^{2}}{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}-d^{*}\xi+\delta x_{i+1}\big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)

in Case
(b)
, where we use the definition of
\widetilde{H}
, as in (
7.3
), in the last step. In Case
(a)
we have
\widetilde{H}(x_{i+1})=H(x_{i+1})
, so that the terms in the first square brackets equal zero by the choice of
H
, and the terms in the second square brackets are positive, since

(7.57)

H(x_{i+1})+x_{i+1}+d^{*}=\sqrt{2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})x_{i+1}}\geq d^{*},

as
x_{i+1}\geq(d^{*})^{2}/(2(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})
in this case. Choosing
\delta,p
, and
\zeta
sufficiently small with respect to
\xi=\xi(\varepsilon)
thus yields the desired result. In Case
(b)
we have directly have that the exponential tends to zero by the choice of
x_{i+1}
in this case and by choosing
\delta
sufficiently small. This concludes the proof of the first result in Lemma
7.6
.

We then consider the probability

(7.58)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(H(u^{*})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big).

In the same manner as we bound the probability in (
7.41
), but replacing
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
with
u^{*}-\varepsilon
and
u^{*}+\varepsilon
, respectively, and setting
k:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(H(u^{*})+\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\rceil
instead, we can follow the same proof to conclude that this probability tends to one as
t
tends to infinity when
p
is sufficiently small with respect to
\xi
(and thus with respect to
\varepsilon
, by (
7.11
)).

Finally, we consider the probability

(7.59)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{\varepsilon,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(d^{*}-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)\rceil,\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big).

Again, in the same manner as we bound the probability in (
7.41
), but replacing
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
with
-\varepsilon
and
0
, respectively, and setting
k:=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(d^{*}-\xi)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\rceil
instead, we can follow the same proof to conclude that this probability tends to one as
t
tends to infinity when
p
is sufficiently small with respect to
\xi
(and thus with respect to
\varepsilon
, by (
7.11
)).
∎

To conclude the section, we prove Lemma
7.8
.

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

We consider the probability

(7.60)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\big(D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)\leq\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)))\rceil\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big),

that all individuals born after time
\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+A\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
that are alive at time
s
have degree at most
\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)))\rceil
for all
s\in F_{p,t}
. We fix
\varepsilon>0
and set
k=\lfloor\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)))\rfloor
and

(7.61)

B_{A,t}:=\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+A\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),t\Big).

We write the probability of interest as

(7.62)

\displaystyle 1{}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\exists s\in F_{p,t}\ \exists v\in B_{A,t}:D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq s-\sigma_{v},S_{D^{(v)}+1}^{(v)}>s-\sigma_{v}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\exists s\in F_{p,t}\ \exists v\in B_{A,t}:D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq s-\sigma_{v}\right).

We bound
s
from above in the second part of the event in the probability. Further, we cover the interval
[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+A\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),t]
by smaller intervals of the form

(7.63)

\Big[\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(j-1)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+j\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big],

where

(7.64)

j\in\Big\{A+1,\ldots,\Big\lceil\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\frac{\lambda^{*}t-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))}{\mathcal{K}(t^{*})}\Big\rceil\Big\}=:I_{A,\varepsilon}.

We also define, for
j\in I_{A,\varepsilon}
,

(7.65)

B_{j,t}:=\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+(j-1)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+j\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

Finally, we introduce the event

(7.66)

\mathcal{E}_{M}:=\big\{\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\leq M\big\},

which holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
when choosing
M=M(\varepsilon)
sufficiently large by Corollary
6.6
. Combining all this, we can bound the probability from below by

(7.67)

1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{E}_{M}^{c})-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\mathcal{E}_{M}\cap\Big\{\exists v\in B_{A,t}:D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-\sigma_{v}\}\bigg).

As stated, we can bound the first probability from above by
\varepsilon
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, by choosing
M
sufficiently large. It thus remains to bound the second probability from above. Using a union bound we obtain the upper bound

(7.68)

\sum_{j\in I_{A,\varepsilon}}\!\!\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{M}\cap\Big\{\exists v\in B_{j,t}:D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq t+p\mathcal{K}(t^{*})-\sigma_{v}\Big\}\right).

We bound each term in the sum separately. We use the fact that
v\in B_{j,t}
to bound
\sigma_{v}
from below to arrive at the upper bound

(7.69)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\mathcal{E}_{M}\cap\bigcup_{v\in B_{j,t}}\Big\{{}

\displaystyle D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*}),\Big\}\bigg).

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{M}
, we can bound the size of
B_{j,t}
from above. Combined with a union bound and using the independence of
D
and
S_{k}
, this yields the upper bound

(7.70)

\displaystyle M{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t+\tfrac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))+j\lambda^{*}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\right).

We apply a Chernoff bound to the second probability on the second line, for some
\theta>0
to be determined, to bound the second line from above by

(7.71)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)+\theta}\bigg)\exp\Big(\theta\big[t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big]\Big).

By applying the bound
\log(1-x)\leq-x-x^{2}/2
for
x\in[0,1)
and similar to (
6.34
), we have

(7.72)

\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)+\theta}\leq\exp\Big(-\theta\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\Big).

Combined with Lemma
5.4
, we can thus bound (
7.71
) from above by

(7.73)

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\theta\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\big)-\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)-\theta\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\Big).

Since
k=\lceil\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t)))\rceil
and
\varphi_{1}
is increasing, we can write this as

(7.74)

\displaystyle\exp{}

\displaystyle\Big(-\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)-\theta(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)-\frac{(j-1-p)^{2}\mathcal{K}(t^{*})^{2}}{2\varphi_{2}(k)}\Big),

where the second step optimises over
\theta
by taking
\theta=(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})/\varphi_{2}(k)
. Further, we write
\rho_{1}(k)=d^{*}\varphi_{1}(k)+\alpha(k)
and use
(c)
in Lemma
5.13
and Assumption
\mathcal{K}
to conclude that
\rho_{2}(k)=((d^{*})^{2}+o(1))\mathcal{K}(t^{*})
. For Case
(2)
in the statement of Proposition
7.1
, we apply
(a)
and
(d)
in Lemma
5.13
and Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
to the term
\alpha(k)
to arrive at

(7.75)

\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{*}d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}t-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}(j-1-p)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\Big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

In Case
(1)
, we have
\rho_{1}(k)=\alpha(k)=\mathcal{O}(1)
without the need for Assumption
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
, so that the upper bound remains valid in Case
(1)
. Multiplying this with the remaining terms on the first line of (
7.70
) yields

(7.76)

M\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\exp\Big(-\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}(j-1-p)^{2}-j\lambda^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}+o(1)\Big]\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\Big).

Observe that this upper bound is summable in
j
and tends to zero with
t
. We now choose
A\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that
\tfrac{1}{2}(j-p-1)^{2}-j\lambda^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}(d^{*})^{2}
is strictly positive and increasing in
j
on
[A,\infty)
. Using this in (
7.68
), we thus obtain for some
c>0
,

(7.77)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{A,\varepsilon}}\!\!\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{M}\cap\Big\{\exists v\in B_{j,t}:D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}<t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))-(j-1-p)\mathcal{K}(t^{*})\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathcal{K}(t^{*})}.

This completes the proof, as we then have for this choice of
A
that

(7.78)

\mathbb{P}\big(D^{\max}_{A,t}(s)\leq\varphi_{1}^{-1}\big(t^{*}-\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(r(t))\big),\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,t}\big)\geq 1-\varepsilon-\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathcal{K}(t^{*})},

and
\varepsilon
is arbitrary. ∎

8.
Lack of persistence in the ‘rich die young’ regime

In this section we analyse the random variable
I^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
, the birth-time of the oldest individual that has the largest offspring at time
t
in the ‘rich die old’ regime. Unlike the previous section, where we proved a precise scaling limit for
I_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
in the ‘rich are old’ regime when the death rates converge to a constant, we only provide a lower bound for
I_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
in this section. However, this lower bound holds in a general setting where few assumptions on the birth and death rates are required.

Recall
R
from (
2.13
) and
\lambda^{*}
from Assumption (
Ma
). Set

(8.1)

\underline{d}:=\liminf_{i\to\infty}d(i)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{d}:=\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i),

and, for a large constant
A>0
, define the function

(8.2)

w(k):=\begin{cases}\varphi_{2}(k)&\mbox{if }\lim_{j\to\infty}\varphi_{2}(j)=\infty,\\
A&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Furthermore, fix
K,K^{\prime}>0
, set
k:=\lceil\rho_{1}^{-1}(K^{\prime}\log t)\rceil
, and define

(8.3)

F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}:=[t-w(k),t+w(k)]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ B_{K,t}:=\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t,\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t\Big).

When
\underline{d}>R
, it follows from Proposition
6.7
that the oldest alive individual at time
t
is an element of
B_{K,t}
. We have the following result, which tells us that no individual in
B_{K,t}
is alive and additionally has a ‘large’ offspring at time
s
, for any
s\in F_{K^{\prime},t}^{*}
.

Proposition 8.1

(Oldest alive individuals do not have large offspring)
.

Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied, that
b
tends to infinity, and that
\underline{d}>R
. For any
K>0
there exists
K^{\prime}>0
such that

(8.4)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in F_{K^{\prime},t}^{*}\ \exists v\in B_{K,t}:v\ \mathrm{alive}\ \mathrm{at}\ \mathrm{time}\ s,\ \deg^{(v)}(s)\geq\lceil\rho_{1}^{-1}(K^{\prime}\log t)\rceil\right)=0.

Remark 8.2
.

Whilst
\rho_{1}
need not be invertible in general (when
d(i)=0
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
), since
\underline{d}>0
in Proposition
8.3
, it follows that
\rho_{1}
is invertible on
[C,\infty)
, where
C
is a sufficiently large constant, so that the quantity
k
is well-defined for all large enough
t
.
\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

As we can bound the birth-time of individuals
v\in B_{K,t}
from above and below, we obtain the upper bound

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}({}

\displaystyle\forall s\in F_{K^{\prime},t}^{*}\ \exists v\in B_{K,t}:v\text{ alive at time }s,\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\bigcap_{s\in F_{K^{\prime},t}^{*}}\bigcup_{v\in B_{K,t}}\!\!\!\{D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq s-\sigma_{v},S_{D^{(v)}+1}^{(v)}>s-\sigma_{v}\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Bigg(\!\bigcup_{v\in B_{K,t}}\!\!\!\!\!\Big\{D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t+w(k),S_{D^{(v)}+1}^{(v)}>\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big\}\!\Bigg).

By conditioning on
|B_{K,t}|
, a union bound yields the upper bound

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg({}

\displaystyle|B_{K,t}|\geq\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+\lambda^{*}(K+\delta)\log t\Big)\bigg)+\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+\lambda^{*}(K+\delta)\log t\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t+w(k),S_{D+1}>\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\right).

The probability on the first line tends to zero with
t
by (
6.53
) in Corollary
6.6
(using
r=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t
,
s=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t
, and
u=\delta\log t
). We further split the probability on the second line in two terms, namely

(8.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}<\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k),S_{D+1}>\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k),\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t+w(k)\Big]\right).

We bound both terms from above, starting with the one on the first line. As
\underline{d}>R
, there exists
I\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(i)>R-1/t
for all
i\geq I
and all
t>0
. As
k\geq I
for all
t
sufficiently large, we can use Lemmas
5.4
and
5.9
to bound the first term on the right-hand side from above by

(8.8)

\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}<\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\}}\exp\Big(\big(R-\tfrac{1}{t}\big)\Big[S_{k}-\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big)\Big]\Big)\right].

We omit the indicator to arrive at the upper bound

(8.9)

\exp\bigg(-\rho_{1}(k)-\big(R-\tfrac{1}{t}\big)\Big(\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big)\bigg)(Rt)^{|[k-1]_{R}|}\!\!\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
i\not\in[k-1]_{R}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}\!\!\!\!\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)-R+1/t},

where we recall
[k-1]_{R}
from (
6.17
) as the set of indices
i\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
such that
b(i)+d(i)=R
.
We bound, using that
1+x\leq\mathrm{e}^{x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(8.10)

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
i\not\in[k-1]_{R}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}\!\!\!\!\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)-R+1/t}\leq\exp\bigg(R\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
i\not\in[k-1]_{R}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}\!\!\!\!\frac{1}{b(i)+d(i)-R}\bigg)=\exp\big(R\varphi_{1}(k)+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}(k))\big),

where the final step uses that
b
tends to infinity. Furthermore, we have
\varphi_{2}=o(\varphi_{1})
and
\varphi_{1}(k)\leq\rho_{1}(k)/(\underline{d}-\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon>0
and
k
large by
(a)
and
(b)
in Lemma
5.13
, since
b
tends to infinity. Additionally, by the choice of
w
, it follows that
w(k)=o(\rho_{1}(k))
. Combining this with (
8.10
) in (
8.9
), we arrive at the upper bound

(8.11)

\exp\bigg(-\Big(1-\frac{R}{\underline{d}-\varepsilon}+o(1)\Big)\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{\lambda^{*}R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+[RK+|[k-1]_{R}|+o(1)]\log t\Big).

As
\underline{d}>R
, it follows that
[k-1]_{R}\leq L
for some constant
L\in\mathbb{N}
, uniformly in
k
. We can further use the choice of
k
and the fact that
\rho_{1}
is increasing to finally obtain

(8.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{}

\displaystyle\Big(D\geq k,S_{k}<\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k),S_{D+1}>\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{*}R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+\big[RK+L-\big(1-\tfrac{R}{\underline{d}-\varepsilon}\big)K^{\prime}+o(1)\big]\log t\Big).

We then bound the term on the second line of (
8.7
) from above. We relax the upper bound on
S_{k}
, again use Lemma
5.4
, and apply a Chernoff bound with parameter
\theta:=R-1/t
, to obtain

(8.13)

\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}>\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\right)\leq\exp\bigg(-\rho_{1}(k)-\theta\Big[\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big]\bigg)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\theta S_{k}}\right].

By the choice of
\theta
, we thus arrive at

(8.14)

\exp\bigg(-\rho_{1}(k)-\Big(R-\frac{1}{t}\Big)\Big[\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+R}t-K\log t-w(k)\Big]\bigg)(Rt)^{|[k-1]_{R}|}\!\!\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
i\not\in[k-1]_{R}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}\!\!\!\!\frac{b(i)+d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)-R+1/t},

which is the same upper bound as in (
8.9
). The second line of (
8.6
) is thus at most

(8.15)

\exp\bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{*}R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+\big[RK+L-\big(1-\tfrac{R}{\underline{d}-\varepsilon}\big)K^{\prime}+o(1)\big]\log t\bigg).

Multiplied with the exponential term on the first line of (
8.6
), we finally arrive at the upper bound

(8.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\forall{}

\displaystyle s\in F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}\ \exists v\in B_{K,t}:v\text{ alive at time }s,\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(\big[\lambda^{*}(K+\delta)+RK+L-\big(1-\tfrac{R}{\underline{d}-\varepsilon}\big)K^{\prime}+o(1)\big]\log t\bigg)+o(1),

which tends to zero for
K^{\prime}
sufficiently large. ∎

Now that we know that the oldest individuals alive at time
t
do not have large offspring, it remains to show that there are individuals alive at time
t
, born much later than the oldest individual, that do indeed attain such large degrees. We abuse notation to write

(8.17)

\overline{d}(i):=\sup_{j\leq i}d(j)\qquad\text{for }i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

It follows that
\overline{d}:=\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i)=\lim_{i\to\infty}\overline{d}(i)
. We then have the following result.

Proposition 8.3
.

Suppose that
b
and and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied. Recall
R
from (
2.13
),
\underline{d},\overline{d}
from (
8.1
) and
F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}
from (
8.3
). Suppose that
\underline{d}>0
and that
b
tends to infinity, such that
\overline{d}(k)=o(b(k))
and
b(k)=\mathcal{O}(k)
. Finally, assume that
b(k)
and
\overline{d}(k)
are regularly varying in
k
with a non-negative exponent. For any
K,K^{\prime}>0
, with
k:=\lceil\rho_{1}^{-1}(K^{\prime}\log t)\rceil
,

(8.18)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}\ \exists v\in\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t,t\Big):v\ \mathrm{alive}\ \mathrm{at}\ \mathrm{time}\ s,\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k\right)=1.

The combination of Propositions
8.1
and
8.3

I^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}\gg O^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
when
\underline{d}>R
. It remains to translate this result to the quantities
I_{n}
and
O_{n}
to prove Theorem
2.10
, which is carried out in Section
9
.

Proof.

To bound the probability in the proposition statement from below, we only consider individuals in

(8.19)

B_{w,t}:=\mathcal{B}\big(t-\varphi_{1}(k),t-\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)\big).

Note that, since
\underline{d}>0
, it follows that
\varphi_{1}(k)=\mathcal{O}(\rho_{1}(k))=\mathcal{O}(\log t)
by
(b)
in Lemma
5.13
. Similarly, regardless of whether
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity or not, we have
w(k)=o(\varphi_{1}(k))
since
b
diverges and by
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
. As a result,

(8.20)

B_{w,t}\subset\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t,t\Big).

We show that there exist individuals in the set
B_{w,t}
who are alive at time
s
and have produced at least
k
children by time
s
, for all
s\in F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}
. By bounding the birth-times of individuals in
B_{w,t}
and
s
from above and below, we obtain the lower bound

(8.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\forall{}

\displaystyle s\in F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}\ \exists v\in\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t,t\Big):v\text{ alive at time }s,\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Bigg(\bigcup_{v\in B_{w,t}}\Big\{D^{(v)}\geq k,S_{k}^{(v)}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k),S_{D^{(v)}+1}^{(v)}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)\Big\}\Bigg).

With a similar approach as in the steps between (
6.75
) and (
6.81
) and by leveraging the independence of the reproduction processes of distinct individuals (when not conditioning on survival), we obtain for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
the lower bound

(8.22)

1-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|B_{w,t}|\leq m\right)-\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)^{-1}\big(1-\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k),S_{D+1}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)\right)\big)^{m}.

We now set

(8.23)

m:=\exp\big(\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}w(k))\big),

and use that
1-x\leq\mathrm{e}^{-x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
to obtain the lower bound

(8.24)

\displaystyle 1{}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}\exp\Big(-\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k),S_{D+1}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{1}{2}w(k))}\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|B_{w,t}|\leq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}w(k))\big)\right).

We first bound the probability on the first line from below and later show that the term on the second line can be made arbitrarily small. To this end, we condition on the event
\{D\geq k\}
and the random variable
S_{k}
to arrive at

(8.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({}

\displaystyle D\geq k,S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k),S_{D+1}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}<\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k)\}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)-S_{k}\,\Bigg|\,S_{k}\right)\right].

We bound the expected value from below by further restricting the value of
S_{k}
to the interval
\mathcal{I}_{k}:=(\varphi_{1}(k)-3w(k),\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k))
in the indicator and then bounding
S_{k}
from below by
\varphi_{1}(k)-3w(k)
in the probability. This yields the lower bound

(8.26)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>4w(k)\right).

To bound the third probability from below, we distinguish between the cases
\overline{d}<\infty
and
\overline{d}=\infty
.

\bm{\overline{d}<\infty.}
There exists
C_{1}\geq\overline{d}
such that
d(i)\leq C_{1}
for all
i\geq k
, so that Lemma
5.5
yields

(8.27)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>4w(k)\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-4C_{1}w(k)}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-C_{2}\overline{d}(k)w(k)},

where
C_{2}>0
is large enough so that
C_{2}\overline{d}(k)\geq C_{1}
for
k
large, which is possible since
\overline{d}(k)
converges to
\overline{d}
as
k
tends to infinity.

\bm{\overline{d}=\infty.}
We recall
\overline{d}(i)
from (
8.17
) and introduce the random variables
(\overline{E}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
\overline{D}_{k}
, where

(8.28)

\overline{E}_{i}\sim\text{Exp}(b(i)+\overline{d}(i))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{P}\!\left(\overline{D}_{k}\geq\ell\right)=\prod_{i=k}^{k+\ell-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+\overline{d}(i)}.

Since
d(i)\leq\overline{d}(i)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it follows that
E_{i}\succeq\overline{E}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
D_{k}\succeq\overline{D}_{k}
. Hence,

(8.29)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>4w(k)\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\overline{d}(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+\overline{D}_{k}}\overline{E}_{i}>4\overline{d}(k)w(k)\right).

We apply Lemma
5.6
to the probability on the right-hand side, i.e. when using the sequences
(b(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(\overline{d}(k))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, for which we need to check several conditions. First, we note that
\overline{d}(k)
is increasing by definition and tends to infinity with
k
since
\overline{d}=\infty
. Then, by assumption
\overline{d}(k)
is regularly varying with non-negative exponent (and increasing), and
\overline{d}(k)=o(b(k))
. Since
b(k)=\mathcal{O}(k)
and
\overline{d}(k)
tends to infinity, it follows that
b(k)=o(k\overline{d}(k))
. Finally, we show that
t_{k}:=4\overline{d}(k)w(k)
satisfies the required conditions. As
w(k)\geq A
for all
k
sufficiently large and
\overline{d}(k)
tends to infinity with
k
, it follows that
\liminf_{k\to\infty}t_{k}>0
. Then, recall that
b
is regularly varying with non-negative exponent and that
\overline{d}(k)=o(b(k))
. If
b
and
d
are such that
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity,

(8.30)

w(k)=\varphi_{2}(k)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{(b(i)+d(i))^{2}}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{b(i)^{2}}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{k}{b(k)^{2}}\Big).

As a result,
t_{k}=\mathcal{O}(k\overline{d}(k)/b(k)^{2})=o(k\overline{d}(k)/b(k))
, where the final step holds since
b
tends to infinity. When, instead,
\varphi_{2}
converges, then
t_{k}=4A\overline{d}(k)=o(kd(k)/b(k))
when
b(k)=o(k)
. As a result, in both cases we obtain the lower bound

(8.31)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\overline{d}(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+\overline{D}_{k}}\overline{E}_{i}>4\overline{d}(k)w(k)\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-(4+o(1))\overline{d}(k)w(k)}.

When
\varphi_{2}
converges but
b(k)=\mathcal{O}(k)
rather than
b(k)=o(k)
, we use Remark
A.1
to obtain a weaker lower bound which holds under the weaker assumption
t_{k}=\mathcal{O}(\overline{d}(k))
, which is the case when
t_{k}=4A\overline{d}(k)
regardless of the value of
A
. This weaker lower bound yields

(8.32)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\overline{d}(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+\overline{D}_{k}}\overline{E}_{i}>4\overline{d}(k)w(k)\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-C_{3}\overline{d}(k)w(k)},

for some large constant
C_{3}>4
. It thus follows from Lemma
5.6
(and Remark
A.1
) that, with
C_{4}=\max\{C_{2},C_{3}\}
,
in both cases
\overline{d}<\infty
and
\overline{d}=\infty
we arrive the lower bound

(8.33)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>4w(k)\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-C_{4}\overline{d}(k)w(k)},

for
k
sufficiently large. Using this in (
8.26
), we arrive at

(8.34)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>4w(k)\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-C_{4}\overline{d}(k)w(k)}.

To conclude the proof, we distinguish between whether
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity or converges.

\bm{\varphi_{2}}
tends to infinity.
It follows from (
8.2
) that
w(k)=\varphi_{2}(k)
. By Lemma
5.10
,

(8.35)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in(\varphi_{1}(k)-3\varphi_{2}(k),\varphi_{1}(k)-2\varphi_{2}(k))\right)=\exp\big(-(2+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)\big).

Combined with Lemma
5.4
and using that
\rho_{2}=o(\rho_{1})
since
d=o(b)
by
(a)
in Lemma
5.13
, we can thus write the lower bound in (
8.34
) as

(8.36)

\exp\big(-(1+o(1))\rho_{1}(k)-(2+o(1))\varphi_{2}(k)-C_{4}\overline{d}(k)\varphi_{2}(k)\big)=\exp\big(-(1+o(1))\rho_{1}(k)),

where we use that
b
tends to infinity and is regularly varying with non-negative exponent and that
\overline{d}(k)=o(b(k))
, so that
\varphi_{2}(k)=\mathcal{O}(\overline{d}(k)\varphi_{2}(k))=o(\varphi_{1}(k))
, and
\varphi_{1}(k)=\mathcal{O}(\rho_{1}(k))
since
\underline{d}>0
by
(a)
and
(b)
in Lemma
5.13
. By the choice of
k
, we thus arrive at the lower bound

(8.37)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-2w(k),S_{D+1}>\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-(K^{\prime}+o(1))\log t}.

Using this in (
8.24
) yields the lower bound

(8.38)

\displaystyle 1{}

\displaystyle-\exp\Big(-\exp\big(-(K^{\prime}+o(1))\log t+\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(k))\big)\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|B_{w,t}|\leq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(k))\big)\right)

Again using that
\varphi_{2}=o(\varphi_{1})
and
\varphi_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\rho_{1})
, so that
\varphi_{1}(k)=o(t)
and
\varphi_{2}(k)=o(t)
, the exponential term equals
\exp(-\exp((\lambda^{*}+o(1))t))
, which tends to zero with
t
. It remains to show that the probability on the second line tends to zero. Here, we use (
6.52
) in Corollary
6.6
, with
r=t-\varphi_{1}(k)
,
s=t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\varphi_{2}(k)
, and
u=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)
, to arrive at the desired result.

\bm{\varphi_{2}}
converges.
We have

(8.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\in(\varphi_{1}(k)-3A,\varphi_{1}(k)-2A)\right)=p_{A}+o(1),

for some constant
p_{A}\in(0,1)
. Indeed,
S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)=S_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]
converges almost surely in this case, as it is a martingale whose quadratic variation equals
\varphi_{2}
, which converges by assumption. Again, combined with Lemma
5.4
, this yields

(8.40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{}

\displaystyle(D\geq k,S_{k}<t-w(k)-(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+w(k)),S_{D+1}>t+w(k)-(t-\varphi_{1}(k)))

\displaystyle\geq\exp\big(-(1+o(1))\rho_{1}(k)-AC_{4}\overline{d}(k)\big).

Using this in (
8.24
), we arrive at the lower bound

(8.41)

\displaystyle 1{}

\displaystyle-\exp\Big(-\exp\big(-(1+o(1))\rho_{1}(k)-AC_{4}\overline{d}(k)+\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+2A)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|B_{w,t}|\leq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}A)\big)\right).

As in the first case,
\varphi_{1}(k)=o(t)
. Furthermore, by the assumption that
\overline{d}(k)=o(b(k))=\mathcal{O}(k)
it follows that
\overline{d}(k)=o(t)
by the choice of
k
, irrespective of the value of
K^{\prime}
. The exponential term on the first line thus tends to zero with
t
independently of the choice of
A
and
K^{\prime}
. To bound the probability on the second line, we write

(8.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\big(|B_{w,t}|\leq\exp\big(\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}A)\big)\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,t-\varphi_{1}(k)+A)|-|\mathcal{B}(t-\varphi_{1}(k),t-\varphi_{1}(k)+A)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+\frac{1}{2}A)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,t-\varphi_{1}(k)+A)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k)+A)}\leq 2\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{*}A}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(|\mathcal{B}(0,t-\varphi_{1}(k))|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}(t-\varphi_{1}(k))}\geq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{*}A}\right),

where the final step uses a union bound. We then bound the right-hand side from above by

(8.43)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\inf_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}t}\leq 2\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{*}A}\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}t}\geq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{*}A}\Big).

We can bound either term from above by
\varepsilon/2
when choosing
A=A(\varepsilon)
sufficiently large by using (
6.54
) and (
6.55
) in Proposition
6.5
. Combining both cases, we thus arrive at

(8.44)

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in F^{*}_{K^{\prime},t}\ \exists v\in\mathcal{B}\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}t+K\log t,t\Big):v\ \mathrm{alive}\ \mathrm{at}\ \mathrm{time}\ s,\deg^{(v)}(s)\geq k\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we arrive the desired result and conclude the proof.
∎

9.
Proofs of the main results

In this section we combine the results proved in Sections
6
,
7
, and
8
(Propositions
6.7
,
7.1
,
8.1
, and
8.3
in particular) to prove the main results presented in Section
2.2
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Recall that
\tau_{n}
, as in (
3.14
), is the stopping time at which exactly
n
many births or deaths have occurred in the branching process
\mathrm{BP}
. Also, recall that
\mathcal{B}(0,t)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}
denote the number of births that occur in
\mathrm{BP}
up to time
t
and the number of alive individuals in
\mathrm{BP}(t)
, respectively. By Proposition
6.5
and Equation (
3.13
), there exist random variables
W_{1},W_{2}
such that

(9.1)

|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}W_{1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ |\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{*}\!t}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}W_{2}.

Moreover,
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
are strictly positive almost surely conditionally on the event
\mathcal{S}
that the branching process survives. If we let
N(t)
denote the the number of births and deaths that occur up to time
t
, then we observe that

(9.2)

N(t)=|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|+(|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|-\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}),\qquad t\geq 0.

Note that
N(t)\geq 0
for all
t\geq 0
, and that
N(t)
is increasing in
t
. Since
(|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|)_{t\geq 0}
and
|(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}|)_{t\geq 0}
are cádlág, it follows that
N(\tau_{n})=n
. Hence,

(9.3)

\tau_{n}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log(2W_{1}-W_{2}).

As
W_{2}\leq W_{1}
since
|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{t}|\leq|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|
and
W_{2}>0
almost surely conditionally on
\mathcal{S}
, the limit is finite
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
-almost surely. Since we assume that
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
diverge, and thus that
\mathcal{K}
diverges, conditionally on
\mathcal{S}
, the event

(9.4)

\tau_{n}\in\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n-p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n+p\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big]=:F_{p,n}^{\prime},

holds with high probability as
n
tends to infinity, for any fixed
p>0
. Then,

(9.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n\big)}\Big(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}\log n-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\Big)\bigg|<\varepsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n\big)}\Big(O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}\log n-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\Big)\bigg|<\varepsilon,\forall s\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\!\bigg)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\tau_{n}\not\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\right).

Applying Proposition
6.7
((
6.66
) in particular) with
t=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n
to the first probability on the right-hand side, with
p
sufficiently small, and (
9.4
) to the final probability yields that the left-hand side converges to one as
n
tends to infinity. We now define, for ease of writing,

(9.6)

\ell_{n}(c):=\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n+\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)+c\lambda^{*}\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n\big),

where
c\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant. We then write the event

(9.7)

\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)}\Big(\log O_{n}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\Big)\bigg|\leq 2\lambda^{*}\varepsilon\bigg\}

as the event

(9.8)

\displaystyle\big\{O_{n}\in\big[\exp\big(\ell_{n}(-2\varepsilon)\big),\exp\big(\ell_{n}(2\varepsilon)\big)\big]\big\}.

The random variable
O_{n}
equals the label of the oldest alive vertex in
T_{n}
, whereas
O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}
equals the birth-time of the oldest alive vertex in
\mathrm{BP}(\tau_{n})
. Since
O_{n}\overset{d}{=}N(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}})
(see (
3.18
)), we thus need to know the number of births and deaths that have occurred in
\mathrm{BP}(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}})
. Hence, we bound

(9.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg({}

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)}\Big(\log O_{n}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\Big)\bigg|\leq 2\lambda^{*}\varepsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Big(O_{n}\in\big[\exp\big(\ell_{n}(-2\varepsilon)\big),\exp\big(\ell_{n}(2\varepsilon)\big)\big]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(-\varepsilon),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(\varepsilon)\Big]\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(N\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(-\varepsilon)\Big)\leq\exp\big(\ell_{n}(-2\varepsilon)\big)\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(N\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(\varepsilon)\Big)\geq\exp\big(\ell_{n}(2\varepsilon)\big)\right).

We then note that the first probability on the right-hand side tends to one by (
9.5
), and the other two probabilities tend to zero by Corollary
6.6
. Indeed, as we can bound

(9.10)

|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|\leq N(t)\leq 2|\mathcal{B}(0,t)|,

we take
r=0
,
u=\varepsilon\lambda^{*}\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)
, and
s=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(-\varepsilon)
for the middle probability and
s=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(\varepsilon)
for the last probability on the right-hand side of (
9.9
). This yields (
2.17
).

In a similar manner,

(9.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg({}

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)}\Big(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}\log n-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\Big)-\frac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}\bigg|<\varepsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\Bigg|\frac{I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}-\frac{d^{*}}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+d^{*})}\log n-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)}{\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)}-\frac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}\Bigg|<\varepsilon\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\tau_{n}\not\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\right),

which converges to one as
n
tends to infinity by applying Proposition
7.1
with
t=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n
and
p
sufficiently small, and using (
9.4
). Then, as
I_{n}\overset{d}{=}N(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}})
and using a similar rewriting as in (
9.7
) and (
9.8
),

(9.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\Big(I_{n}\in\big[\exp\big(\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}-2\varepsilon\big)\big),\exp\big(\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}+2\varepsilon\big)\big)\big]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\not\in\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}-\varepsilon\big),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}+\varepsilon\big)\Big]\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(N\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}-\varepsilon\big)\Big)\leq\exp\big(\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}-2\varepsilon\big)\big)\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(N\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}+\varepsilon\big)\Big)\geq\exp\big(\ell_{n}\big(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}+2\varepsilon\big)\big)\right).

The right-hand side tends to zero by (
9.11
) and combining (
9.10
) with Corollary
6.6
(using
r=0
,
u=\varepsilon\mathcal{K}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n)
and
s=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}-\varepsilon)
for the second and
s=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\ell_{n}(\tfrac{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}{2}+\varepsilon)
for the third probability). This yields (
2.18
).

Finally, we let
\deg^{\max}(s):=\max_{v\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{s}}\deg^{(v)}(s)
for brevity. With the same approach, we again have

(9.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Bigg({}

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{\varphi_{1}(\deg^{\max}(\tau_{n}))-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)}{\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n\big)}-\frac{\lambda^{*}-d^{*}}{2}\Bigg|<\varepsilon\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\Bigg|\frac{\varphi_{1}(\deg^{\max}(s))-\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\big(r\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)}{\mathcal{K}\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}+d^{*}}\log n\big)}-\frac{\lambda^{*}-d^{*}}{2}\Bigg|<\varepsilon\text{ for all }s\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\tau_{n}\not\in F_{p,n}^{\prime}\right)

which converges to one as
n
tends to infinity by applying Proposition
7.1
with
t=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n
and
p
sufficiently small, and using (
9.4
). As

(9.14)

\{\varphi_{1}(\max_{v\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}}\deg^{(v)}(\tau_{n})):n\in\mathbb{N}\}\overset{d}{=}\{\varphi_{1}(\max_{v\in\mathcal{A}_{n}}\deg^{(v)}(n)):n\in\mathbb{N}\},

by Proposition
3.2
, we thus arrive at (
2.19
), which concludes the proof.
∎

We then provide a short proof of Theorem
2.3
, as most results follow directly from Theorem
2.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

The results in (
2.16
) directly follow from Theorem
2.5
. It remains to prove (
2.15
). This follows directly from Proposition
6.7
, in particular (
6.67
) (and its proof), which states that there exists some (random) time
T<\infty
such that
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}=O^{\prime}
for all
t\geq T
almost surely conditionally on
\mathcal{S}
, and for some almost surely finite random variable
O^{\prime}
. After
O^{\prime}
amount of time, the branching process has observed
N(O^{\prime})
many births or deaths, so that
O_{n}\to O:=N(O^{\prime})

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
-a.s.
∎

We then prove Theorem
2.10
, which uses a similar approach as the proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

First, by (
9.3
), for any
p>0
the event

(9.15)

\tau_{n}\in\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n-p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n+p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big]=:\widetilde{F}^{\prime}_{p,n}

holds with probability tending to one as
n
tends to infinity. To prove the convergence of
O_{n}
, we apply a similar argument to that in (
9.5
) and (
9.9
), to arrive at

(9.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\bigg(\Big|\frac{\log O_{n}}{\log n}-\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\Big|<2K\lambda^{*}\frac{\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)}{\log n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in\Big[\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(N\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\leq\exp\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\log n-2K\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(N\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\geq\exp\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\log n+2K\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\bigg).

The last two probabilities on the right-hand side tend to zero by using (
9.10
) in combination with Corollary
6.6
(with
r=0
,
u=K\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n)
, and
s=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
in the second and
s=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
in the third probability). We bound the first probability in the same way as in (
9.5
), to arrive at

(9.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\bigg(O_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in\Big[\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}\in\Big[\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big],\forall s\in\widetilde{F}^{\prime}_{p,n}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\tau_{n}\not\in\widetilde{F}_{p,n}^{\prime}\right).

The first probability on the right-hand side tends to one for
K>K_{0}
and
p
sufficiently small by using (
6.65
) in Proposition
6.7
(with
t=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n
). The second probability tends to zero by (
9.15
). As a result, the left-hand side of (
9.16
) tends to one for any
K>K_{0}
, which yields (
2.21
).

We then prove a lower bound for
I_{n}
, by using results for
I^{\mathrm{cont}}_{\tau_{n}}
. We fix
K^{\prime}>0
, recall
w
from (
8.2
), and set

(9.18)

k_{n}:=\lceil\rho_{1}^{-1}\big(K^{\prime}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\big)\rceil\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}:=\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n-w(k_{n}),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n+w(k_{n})\Big].

We recall the constant
K_{0}
from Proposition
6.7
, fix
K_{1}>K_{0}
, and bound

(9.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\Big(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(I_{s}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\text{ for all }s\in\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\tau_{n}\not\in\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}\right).

The final probability is at most
\varepsilon
by choosing the constant
A
in the definition of
w
sufficiently large with respect to
\varepsilon
and using (
9.3
). With
t=\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n
, we claim that Propositions
6.7
,
8.1
, and
8.3
yield that the first probability on the right-hand side converges to one. First, Proposition
6.7
implies that there exists
p>0
such that oldest individual alive at time
s
has a birth-time at least
\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
, for all
s
in the interval

(9.20)

\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n-p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n+p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big].

We then note that
w(k_{n})
is either equal to a constant
A>0
(when
\varphi_{2}
converges) or is equal to
\varphi_{2}(k_{n})
(when
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity), which is negligible compared to
\rho_{1}(k_{n})
, since
\varphi_{2}=o(\varphi_{1})
, as
b
diverges, and
\varphi_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\rho_{1})
since
\underline{d}>0
. As a result, in either case and for any
A,K^{\prime},p>0
it follows that

(9.21)

\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}\subset\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n-p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big),\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n+p\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big]

for all
n
large (i.e. larger than some fixed
N=N(A,K^{\prime},p)\in\mathbb{N}
). Hence, Proposition
6.7
implies that the oldest individual alive at time
s
has a birth-time at least
\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n-K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
, for all
s\in\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}
with high probability.

Then, Proposition
8.1
tells us that, by choosing
K^{\prime}
sufficiently large with respect to
K_{1}
, there does not exist
s\in\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}
and an individual born before time
\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
that is alive at time
s
and has at least
k_{n}
many children. Finally, Proposition
8.3
yields that at any time
s\in\widetilde{F}^{*}_{K^{\prime},n}
there exists an individual born after time
\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
that is alive at time
s
and has at least
k_{n}
many children. Combined, we conclude that the first term on the right-hand side of (
9.19
) indeed converges to one with
n
. For any
K_{1}>K_{0}
, we thus arrive at

(9.22)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\Big(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, the left-hand side is in fact equal to one. Finally, we bound

(9.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}{}

\displaystyle\bigg(\log I_{n}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\log n+2K_{1}\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(I_{\tau_{n}}^{\mathrm{cont}}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(N\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\geq\exp\Big(\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\log n+2K_{1}\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\Big)\bigg).

The first probability on the right-hand side converges to one for any
K_{1}>K_{0}
by (
9.22
). The second probability tends to zero by using (
9.10
) and (
6.53
) in Corollary
6.6
(with
r=0
,
s=\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}(\lambda^{*}+R)}\log n+K_{1}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
, and
u=K_{1}\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)
). We thus arrive at

(9.24)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigg(\log I_{n}\geq\frac{R}{\lambda^{*}+R}\log n+2K_{1}\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\bigg)=1,

for any
K_{1}>K_{0}
. Combined with the fact that the left-hand side of (
9.16
) converges to one for any
K>K_{0}
, we can thus choose
K_{1}>K>K_{0}
to arrive at the desired result, since then

(9.25)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\log\Big(\frac{I_{n}}{O_{n}}\Big)\geq 2(K_{1}-K)\lambda^{*}\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{*}}\log n\big)\right)=1,

and thus
I_{n}/O_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}}\infty
, as desired, which concludes the proof.
∎

10.
Converging birth rates and constant death rates: Theorem
2.11

In this section we discuss some of the adaptations to the analysis in Sections
6
through
9
required to prove Theorem
2.11
.

Assumptions.
We observe that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
F-D
) are both satisfied when
b
and
d
converge. Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
and (
D-V
) are not required.

Preliminary results.
As
d
converges to one, it follows from Lemma
5.5
that the remaining lifetime
\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}
is asymptotically distributed as a rate-one exponential random variable (for large
k
). By the upper bound in Lemma
5.9
, with
x=1-\varepsilon
,
t^{\prime}\geq t\geq 0
and
k
large, we thus have

(10.1)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-(1-\varepsilon)t^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-\varepsilon)S_{k}}\right].

The lower bound in Lemma
5.9
yields a lower bound for the probability when changing
1-\varepsilon
to
1+\varepsilon
, for
k
large. Then, as
b
converges to
c
and
d
converges to
1
,

(10.2)

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]}{k}=\frac{\varphi_{1}(k)}{k}=\frac{1+o(1)}{c+1}.

As
S_{k}
is a sum of independent exponential random variables whose rates converge to
c+1
, it is clear (by comparing with the case of constant rates equal to
c+1
) that
S_{k}
satisfies a large deviation principle rate function
I
defined as

(10.3)

I(\alpha):=\alpha(c+1)-1-\log(\alpha(c+1))\qquad\text{for }\alpha>0.

That is, for
\alpha<\frac{1}{c+1}
so that
\alpha k<\mathbb{E}\left[S_{k}\right]
for all
k
large (resp.
\alpha>\frac{1}{c+1}
), we have

(10.4)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq\alpha k\right)=-I(\alpha)\qquad\bigg(\text{resp.\ }\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\geq\alpha k\right)=-I(\alpha)\bigg).

Similar to the moderate deviation principle with exponential tilting in Proposition
5.12
, one can prove a large deviation principle with exponential tilting in this setting, in the sense that

(10.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq\alpha k\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta S_{k}}\right]=-I(\alpha)+\alpha\theta\qquad\text{for }\alpha<\frac{1}{c+1},\theta\in\mathbb{R},

and

(10.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\geq\alpha k\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta S_{k}}\right]=-I(\alpha)+\alpha\theta\qquad\text{for }\alpha>\frac{1}{c+1},\theta\in\mathbb{R}.

The oldest alive individual.
As in the ‘rich are old’ and ‘rich die young’ regimes,
L
is approximately distributed as an exponential random variable with rate
\min\{1,R\}
, where we recall that
R:=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(b(i)+d(i))
. More precisely,

(10.7)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)=-\min\{1,R\}=:M.

One can then show, following a proof similar to that of (
6.65
) and (
6.66
) (with
d^{*}=1
) in Proposition
6.7
that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p>0
such that

(10.8)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]:\Big|\frac{O_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}}{t}-\frac{M}{\lambda^{*}+M}\Big|<\varepsilon\right)=1.

The individual with the largest offspring.
Define the constant

(10.9)

u^{*}=u^{*}(\lambda^{*}):=1-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{2(\lambda^{*}+1)\log 2}.

One can then combine the upper bound in (
10.1
) (and the equivalent lower bound where
1-\varepsilon
is replaced with
1+\varepsilon
) with the tilted large deviation principle in (
10.5
) in a proof similar to that of Proposition
7.1
to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p>0
such that

(10.10)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\forall s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]:\Big|\frac{I_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}}{t}-u^{*}\Big|<\varepsilon\text{ and }\Big|\frac{\max_{v\in\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\mathrm{cont}}}d^{(v)}(s)}{t}-\frac{\lambda^{*}}{\log 2}\Big|<\varepsilon\right)=1.

Indeed, we partition
[\frac{M}{\lambda^{*}+M},u^{*}-\varepsilon)\cup(u^{*}+\varepsilon,1]
in small intervals
[x_{i},x_{i+1})
of length
\zeta>0
, for
i\in\mathbb{I}
. Similar to the proof of Proposition
7.1
, one can show that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p>0
such that

(10.11)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(I_{s}\in[u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon]\text{ for all }s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]\right)=1.

To this end, with
\xi>0
small, one is required to show that

(10.12)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(D^{\max}_{u^{*}-\varepsilon,u^{*}+\varepsilon}(s)\geq(H(u^{*})-\xi)t,\text{ for all }s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]\big)=1,

and

(10.13)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(D^{\max}_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}(s)\leq(H(x_{i+1})+\xi)t,\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{I}\text{ and for all }s\in[(1-p)t,(1+p)t]\big)=1,

where
H\colon[(\lambda^{*}+1)^{-1},1)\to[-1-(\lambda^{*}+1)^{-1},\infty)
is defined as

(10.14)

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle:=(\lambda^{*}+1)(1-x)\Big(1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+1}+w^{-1}\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{*}x}{(\lambda^{*}+1)(1-x)}\Big)\Big),

and
w\colon[-(\lambda^{*}+1)^{-1},\infty)\to(-\infty,-(\lambda^{*}+1)^{-1}]
is defined as

(10.15)

\displaystyle w(x)

\displaystyle:=x-\Big(1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+1}+x\Big)\log\Big(1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}+1}+x\Big).

It is readily verified that
H
has a unique maximum at
u^{*}
of value
H(u^{*})=\lambda^{*}/\log 2
.
Finally, using a similar proof as that of Theorem
2.5
yields the asymptotic behaviour of
O_{n}
and
I_{n}
and proves Theorem
2.11
.

Constant birth and death rates (Remark
2.12
).
In the case that
b\equiv c>1
and
d\equiv 1
, by
[
14
, Proposition
1.1
]
(see also Proposition
3.3
),

(10.16)

\widehat{\mu}(\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=0}^{k}\frac{b(i)}{\lambda+b(i)+d(i)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{c}{\lambda+c+1}\Big)^{k+1}=\frac{c}{\lambda+1}.

As a result, Assumption (
Ma
) is satisfied with
\lambda^{*}=c-1>0
and
\underline{\lambda}=0
, so that the general result in Theorem
2.11
follows for this case with
\lambda^{*}=c-1
.

11.
Discussion and open problems

In this article we investigated an evolving tree process where, at each step, either a new alive vertex is introduced that connects to an already present alive vertex, or an alive vertex is selected and killed. Via an embedding in a CMJ branching process, we analysed this process and studied lack of persistence of the maximum degree: under what conditions is the label of the vertex with the largest in-degree much larger than the label of the oldest alive vertex (i.e. the alive vertex with the smallest label)?

We identified the ‘rich are old’ and ‘rich die young’ regimes’, in which lack of persistence occurs for different reasons. In the former regime, where behaviour is somewhat similar to that of preferential attachment without death, lack of persistence occurs due to the fact that one can find individuals (in the branching process) that are slightly older than the oldest alive individuals and both manage to survive and to produce a large number of children, faster than typical individuals. In the latter regime, the oldest individuals manage to survive by only producing a bounded number of children, whereas individuals that produce a large number of children can only survive for a much shorter amount of time. This regime is entirely novel and cannot be observed in preferential attachment without death, and has not been studied nor identified before.

Limitations, conjectures, and open problems.

Though we present a variety of results that provide a good understanding of the phase transition between the ‘rich are old’ and the ‘rich die young’ regimes, there are still cases we are currently not able to deal with, and limitations to the set-up we considered, which we address here.

Behaviour of
I_{n}
in the ‘rich die young’ regime.
The results in the ‘rich die young’ regime, in particular that there is always lack of persistence of the maximum degree, hold for quite a general choice of birth and death rates. Unlike in the ‘rich are old’ regime, however, we do not provide a scaling limit for
I_{n}
. When the death rates converge to a constant
d^{*}\geq R
, a similar result as in Theorem
2.5
holds (see Proposition
7.1
). However, when the death rates diverge, making it much harder for individuals to survive for a long time and also obtain a large offspring, it is not entirely clear to us what the right scaling of
I_{n}
should be. We leave this as an open problem.

Open Problem 11.1
.

Suppose that the sequence
d
tends to infinity. Theorem
2.10
shows that
O_{n}=n^{R/(\lambda^{*}+R)+o(1)}
conditionally on survival. What is the order of
I_{n}
? We expect that
I_{n}=o(n)
but
I_{n}\gg n^{1-\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Bounded non-converging death rates.
The results presented in the ‘rich are old’ regime do not consider the case when the death rates are bounded but do not converge. This is mainly due to the difficulty to determine the lifetime distribution of individuals in complete generality. As an example, let us consider the case of converging birth rates and alternating convergent death rates. That is, take three sequences
(c_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(d_{1,i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(d_{2,i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}c_{i}=c>0
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{j,i}=d_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, with
d_{1}\neq d_{2}\geq 0
. We set

(11.1)

d=(d_{1,1},d_{2,1},d_{1,2},d_{2,2},\ldots),\qquad b(i)=c_{i}.

We then claim (without proof) that the lifetime distribution satisfies

(11.2)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)=-\min\bigg\{c+\frac{d_{1}+d_{2}}{2}-\sqrt{c^{2}+\Big(\frac{d_{1}-d_{2}}{2}\Big)^{2}},R\bigg\}=:-\theta.

This already non-trivial result can be obtained due to the somewhat simple choice of the birth and death rates. More general birth or death rates (e.g.
b
that diverge to infinity) make it much harder to determine the limiting value of
\theta
that characterises the exponential decay of the lifetime distribution.

We expect that in this more general setting, a distinction between the ‘rich are old’ and the ‘rich die young’ regimes can be made depending on whether
\theta<R
or
\theta=R
. We leave this as an open problem.

Open Problem 11.2
.

Suppose the sequences
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied. Assume that
\theta:=\lim_{t\to\infty}-\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(L>t\right)
exists and recall
R
from (
2.13
). Can we define the ‘rich are old’ and the ‘rich die young’ regimes via
\theta<R
and
\theta=R
, respectively?

We recall that
\theta=\min\{d^{*},R\}
in the case that
d
converges to a constant
d^{*}\geq 0
, which indeed agrees with the open problem.

Omitting assumptions.
There are various assumptions used throughout the analysis to prove the results presented here. A number of these assumptions are not necessary to determine whether persistence of lack thereof occurs, we believe. Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
are necessary to obtain higher-order behaviour of the quantities
O_{n}
and
I_{n}
(and their continuous counterparts
O_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
and
I_{t}^{\mathrm{cont}}
), which we use to determine whether lack of persistence occurs. However, we believe that, like the recent developments presented by Iyer in
[
27
]
, it should be possible to prove (lack of) persistence occurs even without knowing the order of
O_{n}
and
I_{n}
. This would deem Assumptions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
unnecessary, as presented in the following conjecture.

Conjecture 11.3
.

Consider the PAVD model, as in Definition
1.1
. Suppose that
b
and
d
are such that Assumptions (
N-E
) and (
Ma
) are satisfied. Recall
R
from (
2.13
) and suppose that
d
satisfies that
\limsup_{i\to\infty}d(i)<R
and that
b
diverges. Then, persistence holds as in the sense of (
P
) when Assumption (
D-V
) is not satisfied, whereas lack of persistence holds as in the sense of (
NP
) when Assumption (
D-V
) is satisfied.

One could possibly go even further and omit Assumption (
Ma
), which guarantees the existence of a
Malthusian parameter
, as well.

Open Problem 11.4
.

Does the statement in Conjecture
11.3
still hold without Assumption (
Ma
)?

Non-tree graphs.
The analysis carried out here considers trees only. It would be interesting to also study evolving graphs with vertex death that are not trees, e.g. when every new vertex connects to
m>1
many alive vertices. The restriction to trees is due to the continuous-time embedding into a CMJ branching process that is central in the analysis, and which naturally restricts us to the setting of trees.

In the case of
affine
preferential attachment graphs without vertex death, a procedure known as
collapsing branching processes
is used to extend the branching process analysis to non-tree graphs as well
[
21
]
. However, this is limited to the affine case only. When including vertex death, this could only have a potential when the death rates are affine as well. Even then, this introduces difficulties due to the fact that now the lifetime of distinct vertices could have dependencies, which further complicates the analysis.

Other modelling choices.
In the (embedding of the) PAVD model the size of the offspring of an individual equals the number of children said individual has produced. Similarly, the rate at which an individual produces its next child or dies depends on the total number of children produced. Instead, one could count only
alive
children towards the offspring, or let the birth and death rates depend on the number of
alive
children. Either way of defining the model can be motivated from applications. However, the analysis for these alternative choices present more challenges, since now either an individual needs to produce
n
children out of which
n-k
should die to have an offspring of size
k
, or the rates depend not only on the parent individual but also on the lifetimes of its children. Deijfen concludes from simulations presented in
[
14
]
that the asymptotic behaviour of the degree distribution should not change significantly, and we expect this to be the case for other properties such as persistence as well. Still, it would be interesting to further investigate these models as well.

Appendix A
Proofs of preliminary results

In this appendix we present the proofs of the preliminary results of Section
5
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

The distribution of
D
follows directly from its definition in (
2.4
) and Definition
5.2
of an inhomogeneous geometric random variable. What remains is to show that
D
is almost surely finite if and only if Assumption (
F-D
) is satisfied. To this end, we write

(A.1)

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=k\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d(k)}{b(k)+d(k)}\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}-\prod_{i=0}^{k}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big).

We thus have a telescopic sum, where the first term in the brackets equals one when
k=0
. As a result, we obtain that
D
is finite almost surely if and only if

(A.2)

\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}=\infty.

Here, the final claim follows directly from the equivalence of the convergence of infinite sums and products. That is, for
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subset[0,1)
,

(A.3)

\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-x_{i})>0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}x_{i}<\infty,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

First, we prove the upper bound. Using the inequality
\log(1-x)\leq-x-x^{2}/2
for
x\in[0,1)
and the tail distribution of
D
, as in (
5.5
), we obtain

(A.4)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\leq\exp\bigg(-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\Big(\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big)^{2}\bigg)=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{2}(k)}.

For the lower bounds, we use that
\log(1-x)\geq-x-x^{2}/2-x^{3}/(1-x)
for
x\in[0,1)
to obtain

(A.5)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\geq\exp\bigg(-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\Big(\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big)^{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\Big(\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big)^{2}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)}\bigg).

In the case that
\rho_{2}
diverges and
d=o(b)
, it follows that the third sum is negligible compared to the second sum (which equals
\rho_{2}(k)
), so that we obtain the lower bound

(A.6)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-\rho_{1}(k)-(\frac{1}{2}+o(1))\rho_{2}(k)},

as desired. In the case that
\rho_{2}
converges, it follows that the fraction
d(i)/(b(i)+d(i))
tends to zero with
i
. As a result, we have
d=o(b)
