, so that the third sum converges as well. We again obtain the desired lower bound, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

We start by proving (
5.12
). First, note that
D_{0}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}D
, so that the result
L\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathrm{Exp}(d^{*})
when
d\equiv d^{*}
directly follows from the case
I=k=0
in (
5.12
). We can interpret the sum in (
5.12
) as follows. We have exponential ‘birth clocks’
(E^{b}_{i})_{i\geq k}
that ring at rates
(b(i))_{i\geq k}
, and exponential ‘death clocks’
(E^{d}_{i})_{i\geq k}
that ring at rate
d^{*}
. All clocks are independent. We first let
E^{b}_{k}
and
E^{d}_{k}
run. If
E^{b}_{k}
rings before
E^{d}_{k}
, we let
E^{b}_{k+1}
and
E^{d}_{k+1}
run. This process continues until some
j\geq k
, where
j
is the first index such that
E^{d}_{j}
rings before
E^{b}_{j}
. This process is equivalent to waiting for the clocks of the sum in (
5.12
) to ring. Indeed,
E_{i}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\min\{E^{b}_{i},E^{d}_{i}\}
and, independently of
E_{i}
,

(A.7)

k+D_{k}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}j:=\inf\{\ell\geq k:E^{d}_{\ell}<E^{b}_{\ell}\}.

The sum in (
5.12
) then is the total time that has passed, that is,
E^{b}_{k}+\cdots+E^{b}_{j-1}+E^{d}_{j}
.

Since all death clocks have the same rate
d^{*}
, by the memoryless property of the exponential distribution, the above process is equivalent to running only one death clock
E^{d}_{k}
, and running the birth clocks
(E^{b}_{i})_{i\geq k}
until
E^{d}_{k}
rings. That is, resampling the death clocks
(E^{d}_{i})_{i\geq k}
every time
E^{b}_{i}
rings first is equivalent to letting
E^{d}_{k}
continue to run and only resampling the clocks
(E^{b}_{i})_{i\geq k}
. It then directly follows that the total time that has passed equals
E^{d}_{k}\sim\text{Exp}(d^{*})
, from which (
5.12
) follows. Assumption (
N-E
) is required to ensure that
v
does not produce an infinite number of children before
E^{d}_{k}
amount of time has passed almost surely. That is, it ensures that
\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}E_{i}^{b}>E^{d}_{k}
almost surely.

We then prove (
5.13
). Let
\widetilde{D}_{k}
be a inhomogeneous geometric random variable characterised by the sequences
(b(i))_{i\geq k}
and
\lambda
, that is,

(A.8)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\widetilde{D}_{k}\geq\ell\right)=\prod_{i=k}^{k+\ell-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+\lambda}\qquad\text{for }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and let
\widetilde{E}_{i}\sim\text{Exp}(b(i)+\lambda)
, independent of
\widetilde{D}_{k}
. Then, as
d(i)\geq\lambda
for all
i\geq k
, it follows that
D_{k}\preceq\widetilde{D}_{k}
and
E_{i}\preceq\widetilde{E}_{i}
for any
i\geq k
. The desired result thus follows by using (
5.12
). The converse result follows mutatis mutandis by reversing inequalities.

Finally, we prove (
5.14
). Since Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied, it follows from Lemma
5.3
that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=\infty\right)>0
. An analogous proof yields that
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}=\infty\right)>0
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then, the case distinction in Table
1
yields that
S_{\infty}=\infty
almost surely when Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied but Assumption (
F-D
) is not. Equivalently,
\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}E_{i}=S_{\infty}-S_{k}=\infty
almost surely. Hence, by the independence of the exponential random variables
E_{i}
and
D_{k}
,

(A.9)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}=\infty\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}E_{i}=\infty\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}=\infty\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}=\infty\right)>0\qquad\text{for any }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Since
d
is increasing, we observe that
d(i)\geq d(k)
for all
i\geq k
. Hence, using Lemma
5.5
, for any
t\geq 0
,

(A.10)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\geq t\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-t},

so that, in particular, we obtain the desired upper bound for
t=t_{k}
. For a lower bound, we fix
\varepsilon>0
and set
\ell_{k}:=\lfloor(1+\varepsilon)t_{k}b(k)/d(k)\rfloor
, to bound

(A.11)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\geq t_{k}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E_{i}\geq t_{k}\bigg)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}\geq\ell_{k}\right).

We show that the first term on the right-hand side tends to one for any
\varepsilon>0
and that the second term is at least
\mathrm{e}^{-(1+2\varepsilon)t_{k}}
. As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, the result then follows. Let us start with the first term. First, since
d
is increasing, the rates of each of the exponential random variables
E_{i}
in the sum are at most
\overline{b}_{k}+d(k+\ell_{k})
, where
\overline{b}_{k}:=\sup\big\{b(i):k\leq i\leq k+\ell_{k}\big\}
. Hence, with
(E^{\prime}_{i})_{i\geq k}
a sequence of i.i.d. rate-one exponential random variables,

(A.12)

\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E_{i}\succeq\frac{1}{\overline{b}_{k}+d(k+\ell_{k})}\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E^{\prime}_{i}.

By assuming that
t_{k}=o(kd(k)/b(k))
, it follows that
\ell_{k}=o(k)
, so that the index
i
in the suprema in the definition of
\overline{b}_{k}
ranges from
k
to
k+o(k)
, and
d(k+\ell_{k})=d(k+o(k))
. As a result, since
b
and
d
are either regularly varying or slowly varying, we obtain from
[
7
, Theorems
1.2.1
and
1.5.2
]
that
\overline{b}_{k}=(1+o(1))b(k)
and
d(k+\ell_{k})=(1+o(1))d(k)
. For any
\xi>0
and
k
sufficiently large, we thus arrive at

(A.13)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E_{i}\geq t_{k}\bigg)\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{d(k)}{(1+\xi)(b(k)+d(k))}\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E_{i}^{\prime}\geq t_{k}\bigg).

The sum on the right-hand side contains
\ell_{k}+1
many i.i.d. random variables with mean one. Note that
\ell_{k}
diverges, since
d=o(b)
and
\liminf_{k\to\infty}t_{k}>0
. Hence, the definition of
\ell_{k}
and the fact that
d=o(b)
, combined with the strong law of large numbers, implies that

(A.14)

\frac{d(k)}{(1+\xi)(b(k)+d(k))t_{k}}\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}}E_{i}^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1+\xi}.

By taking
\xi<\varepsilon
, the right-hand side of (
A.13
) thus converges to one with
k
.

It remains to prove that

(A.15)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}\geq\ell_{k}\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-(1+2\varepsilon)t_{k}}.

By using the distribution of
D_{k}
in (
5.10
) together with the inequality
\log(1-x)\geq-x-x^{2}/2-x^{3}/(1-x)
for
x\in(0,1)
, we obtain the lower bound

(A.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}\geq\ell_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}-1}\frac{b(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}

\displaystyle\geq\exp\bigg(-\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}-1}\!\!\!\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}-1}\!\!\!\Big(\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big)^{2}-\sum_{i=k}^{k+\ell_{k}-1}\!\!\!\Big(\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}\Big)^{2}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)}\bigg).

Equivalent to
\overline{b}_{k}
, we also define
\underline{b}_{k}:=\inf\big\{b(i):k\leq i\leq k+\ell_{k}\big\}
. Also using that
d
is increasing, we obtain the lower bound

(A.17)

\exp\bigg(-\ell_{k}\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\underline{b}_{k}}-\frac{1}{2}\ell_{k}\Big(\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\underline{b}_{k}}\Big)^{2}-\ell_{k}\Big(\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\underline{b}_{k}}\Big)^{3}\bigg).

It again follows from
[
7
, Theorems
1.2.1
and
1.5.2
]
that
\underline{b}_{k}=(1+o(1))b(k)
. Recalling that
d(k+\ell_{k})=(1+o(1))d(k)
and
\liminf_{k\to\infty}t_{k}>0
, we thus have

(A.18)

\ell_{k}\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\overline{b}_{k}}=(1+\varepsilon+o(1))t_{k},\qquad\ell_{k}\Big(\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\overline{b}_{k}}\Big)^{2}=o(t_{k}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell_{k}\Big(\frac{d(k+\ell_{k})}{\overline{b}_{k}}\Big)^{3}=o(t_{k}),

from which we arrive at the final lower bound

(A.19)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}\geq\ell_{k}\right)\geq\exp\big(-(1+\varepsilon+o(1))t_{k}\big)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-(1+2\varepsilon)t_{k}},

when
k
is sufficiently large, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark A.1

(Linear birth rates)
.

We can weaken the assumption that
t_{k}=o(kd(k)/b(k))
somewhat in the case when
b(k)=\mathcal{O}(k)
. Namely, we assume that
t_{k}=\mathcal{O}(d(k))
. This weaker assumption is required in the proof of Proposition
8.3
. If we follow the proof, we then can no longer use that
\ell_{k}=o(k)
, but can only conclude that
\ell_{k}=\mathcal{O}(k)
. As a result, we cannot use that
\underline{b}_{k}=(1+o(1))\overline{b}_{k}=(1+o(1))b(k)
and
d(k+\ell_{k})=(1+o(1))d(k)
, but rather
\underline{b}_{k},\overline{b}_{k}=\Theta(b(k))
and
d(k+\ell_{k})=\Theta(d(k))
. By altering the definition of
\ell_{k}
to
\ell_{k}:=\lfloor Ct_{k}k/d(k)\rfloor
for some sufficiently large constant
C>0
, we can still obtain the lower bound

(A.20)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(d(k)\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}\geq t_{k}\right)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-\widetilde{C}t_{k}},

where
\widetilde{C}>0
is a sufficiently large constant. Though this lower bound does not match with the upper bound provided in the proof, it is sufficient for our purposes when using this result in the proof of Proposition
8.3
.
\blacktriangleleft

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

On the event
\{D\geq k\}
we can write
D=k+D_{k}
, where
D_{k}
is defined in (
5.10
). Using that the exponential random variables
(E_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are independent of
D
, we obtain

(A.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t,S_{k+1+D_{k}}>t^{\prime}\,|\,D\geq k\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t,S_{k+1+D_{k}}>t^{\prime}\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>t^{\prime}-S_{k}\,\Bigg|\,S_{k}\right)\right]\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right).

As
d(i)\geq x
(resp.
d(i)\leq x
), Lemma
5.5
with
\lambda=x
yields the upper bound (resp. lower bound)

(A.22)

\mathrm{e}^{-x(t^{\prime}-t)}\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathrm{e}^{x(S_{k}-t)}\right],

which proves
(i)
(resp.
(ii)
). To prove
(iii)
, we immediately arrive at the upper bound
\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t\right)
by omitting the events
\{D\geq k\}
and
\{S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\}
. To obtain a lower bound, we use the same steps as in (
A.21
) to arrive at

(A.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k,S_{k}\leq t,S_{D+1}>t^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq t\}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}>t^{\prime}-S_{k}\,\Bigg|\,S_{k}\right)\right]\mathbb{P}\!\left(D\geq k\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq t\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+D_{k}}E_{i}=\infty\right)\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=\infty\right).

As Assumption (
F-D
) is not satisfied, Lemma
5.3
yields that the last probability is non-zero. As, additionally, Assumption (
N-E
) is satisfied, (the proof of) Lemma
5.5
yields that the second probability equals
\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{k}=\infty\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(D_{0}=\infty\right)=\mathbb{P}\!\left(D=\infty\right)
, so that we arrive at the desired lower bound and conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.12
.

We start by proving (
5.21
). For
\theta=0
, the result directly follows from Lemma
5.10
, since
{\rm Var}(S_{k})=\varphi_{2}(k)
, which tends to infinity with
k
by Assumption (
D-V
). We then take
\theta>0
. First, we prove an upper bound. By directly bounding
S_{k}
from above by
\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)
and applying Lemma
5.10
, we arrive at the upper bound

(A.24)

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi_{2}(k)}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta(S_{k}-(\varphi_{1}(k)-y\varphi_{2}(k)))}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta(y-z)+\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varphi_{2}(k)}\log\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)\right)=\theta(y-z)-\frac{z^{2}}{2}.

We then prove a lower bound. We fix
\varepsilon>0
and bound

(A.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{}

\displaystyle\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta(S_{k}-(\varphi_{1}(k)-y\varphi_{2}(k)))}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{(S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k))/\varphi_{2}(k)\in(-(z+\varepsilon),-z]\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta(S_{k}-(\varphi_{1}(k)-y\varphi_{2}(k)))}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\!\left(\frac{S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)}{\varphi_{2}(k)}\in(-(z+\varepsilon),-z]\right)\mathrm{e}^{\theta(y-(z+\varepsilon))\varphi_{2}(k))}.

We write the probability as

(A.26)

\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)\leq-z\varphi_{2}(k)\right)-\mathbb{P}\!\left(S_{k}-\varphi_{1}(k)\leq-(z+\varepsilon)\varphi_{2}(k)\right).

We then apply Lemma
5.10
to bound the first probability from below and the second probability from above, to obtain, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and all
k
sufficiently large, the lower bound

(A.27)

\exp\Big(-\frac{(z+\varepsilon/3)^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\Big)-\exp\Big(-\frac{(z+2\varepsilon/3)^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\Big)=(1-o(1))\exp\Big(-\frac{(z+\varepsilon/3)^{2}}{2}\varphi_{2}(k)\Big).

All combined, we thus arrive at

(A.28)

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi_{2}(k)}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{S_{k}\leq\varphi_{1}(k)-z\varphi_{2}(k)\}}\mathrm{e}^{\theta(S_{k}-(\varphi_{1}(k)-y\varphi_{2}(k)))}\right]\geq\theta(y-(z+\varepsilon))-\frac{(z+\varepsilon/3)^{2}}{2}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we arrive at the desired result. The case
\theta<0
follows mutatis mutandis, which concludes the proof of (
5.21
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.13
.

The proofs are based on the following. Suppose that
\ell\colon\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f\colon\mathbb{N}_{0}\to(0,\infty)
are functions such that
\ell
tends to zero and

(A.29)

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{f(i)}=\infty.

Then,

(A.30)

\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{\ell(i)}{f(i)}=o\bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{f(i)}\bigg)\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

Indeed, fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
i_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
|\ell(i)|<\varepsilon
for all
i\geq i_{\varepsilon}
. Then, for
ki_{\varepsilon}
,

(A.31)

\bigg|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{\ell(i)}{f(i)}\bigg|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\varepsilon}-1}\frac{|\ell(i)|}{f(i)}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=i_{\varepsilon}}^{k-1}\frac{1}{f(i)}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\varepsilon}-1}\frac{|\ell(i)|}{f(i)}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{f(i)}.

As a result, by (
A.29
) and since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary we arrive at (
A.30
). Similarly, when
\ell(i)
tends to infinity with
i
,

(A.32)

\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{f(i)}=o\bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{\ell(i)}{f(i)}\bigg)\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

We now prove the lemma.

(a)
By Assumption (
N-E
), we have that
\varphi_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{1}^{-1}(x)
both tend to infinity with
x
. If
\varphi_{2}
converges, both claims are immediate. If
\varphi_{2}
tends to infinity, we use that
b
diverges and apply (
A.30
) with
\ell(i):=1/(b(i)+d(i))
and
f(i):=b(i)+d(i)
. For the second part, we apply (
A.30
) with
\ell(i):=d(i)/(b(i)+d(i))
and
f(i):=(b(i)+d(i))/d(i)
.

(b)
Again, we have that
\varphi_{1}(x)
tends to infinity with
x
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
i_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
d(i)\leq\overline{d}+\varepsilon
for all
i\geq i_{\varepsilon}
. Hence, for
k>i_{\varepsilon}
,

(A.33)

\rho_{1}(k)=\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\varepsilon}-1}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}+(\overline{d}+\varepsilon)\sum_{i=i_{\varepsilon}}^{k-1}\frac{1}{b(i)+d(i)}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\varepsilon}-1}\frac{d(i)}{b(i)+d(i)}+(\overline{d}+\varepsilon)\varphi_{1}(k).

As
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we arrive at the first result. The second is immediate, since
\rho_{1}(k)/\varphi_{1}(k)\geq 0
. The second part of
(b)
follows in an analogous way.

(c)
By Assumption (
D-V
), we have that
\varphi_{2}(x)
tends to infinity with
x
. We then apply (
A.30
) with
\ell(i)=d(i)^{2}-(d^{*})^{2}
and
f(i):=1/(b(i)+d(i))^{2}
.

(d)
By Assumption (
N-E
), we have that
\varphi_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{1}^{-1}(x)
both tend to infinity with
x
. Further, we have
\alpha=o(\varphi_{1})
, which follows from (
A.30
) with
\ell(i):=d(i)-d^{*}
and
f(i):=1/(b(i)+d(i))
. As
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)=\alpha(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t))
we thus obtain the first result. The second result follows as for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
t_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
t\geq t_{\varepsilon}
,

(A.34)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t)-\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}(t-s(t))|\leq\int_{\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t-s(t))}^{\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t)}\!\frac{|d(\lfloor x\rfloor)-d^{*}|}{b(\lfloor x\rfloor)+d(\lfloor x\rfloor)}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\varepsilon\int_{\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t-s(t))}^{\varphi_{1}^{-1}(t)}\!\frac{1}{b(\lfloor x\rfloor)+d(\lfloor x\rfloor)}\,\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon s(t).

The last step follows from the definition of
\varphi_{1}
(and the extension of its domain to
(0,\infty)
by linear interpolation). As
\varepsilon
arbitrary, we arrive at the desired result.
∎
